\chapteropen[\href{https://www.hodinkee.com/articles/patek-philippe-caliber-89-easter-problem}{The astronomical clock in Notre-Dame-de-Strasbourg Cathedral, for computing the date of Easter.  Easter falls on the first Sunday after the full moon on or after the spring equinox. Calculation of this date was a great challenge for mechanisms of that time, 1843.}]{automata/pix/easterclock.jpg}{Automata} %https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:CIMA_mg_8332.jpg
% Use as much of http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14818/4731 as possible

Our touchstone model of mechanical computation is the Turing machine.
A Turing machines has only two components, a CPU and a tape.
We will now take the tape away and
study the CPU alone.

Alternatively stated,
while a Turing Machine has unbounded memory, 
the devices that we use every day do not.
We can ask \citetext{what jobs can be done by a machine with bounded memory}{%
  From Rabin, Scott, Finite Automata and Their Decision Problems, 1959:
  \textit{Turing machines are widely considered to be the abstract prototype
  of digital computers; workers in the field, however, have felt more and more
  that the notion of a Turing machine is too general to serve as an accurate
  model of actual computers. It is well known that even for simple
  calculations it is impossible to give an a priori upper bound on the amount
  of tape a Turing machine will need for any given computation.
  It is precisely this feature that renders Turing's concept unrealistic.
  % 
  In the last few years the idea of a finite automaton has appeared in the
  literature. These are machines having only a finite number of internal
  states that can be used for memory and computation. The restriction on
  finiteness appears to give a better approximation to the idea of a physical
  machine. Of course, such machines cannot do as much as Turing machines,
  but the advantage of being able to compute an arbitrary general recursive
  function is questionable, since very few of these functions come up in
  practical applications.} % http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14816
  }.



\section{Finite State Machines}\index{Finite State machine|(}
We produce a new model of computation by modifying
the definition of Turing Machine.
We will strip out the capability to write, changing the
tape head from read/write to read-only.
This gives us insight into what can be done with states alone.
It will turn out that this type of machine can do many things, but
not as many as a Turing machine.

\subsection{Definition}  
We will use the same type of transition tables and transition graphs
as with Turing machines.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PowerSwitch}
A power switch has two states, $q_{\text{off}}$ and $q_{\text{on}}$ and
its input alphabet has one symbol, \str{toggle}.

\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:Turnstile}
Operate this turnstile by puting in two tokens and then pushing through.
It has three states and
its input alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{token},\str{push}}$.
\begin{center}
  % \newlength{\scratchheight}
  % \newsavebox{\scratchbox}
  \savebox{\scratchbox}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa01.pdf}}
  \settoheight{\jhscratchheight}{\usebox{\scratchbox}}
  \vcenteredhbox{\usebox{\scratchbox}}
  \hspace{5em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.925\jhscratchheight]{automata/pix/turnstile2.jpg}} % http://turnstiles.us/hwd/catrax_plus_foto3.jpg % http://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/Turnstile.alewife.agr.jpg
\end{center}
\end{example}

As we saw with Turing machines, the states are a limited form of memory.
For instance, $q_{\text{one}}$ is how the turnstile ``remembers'' that it
has so far received one token.

\begin{example} \label{ex:VendingMachine}
This vending machine dispenses items that cost $30$~cents.\footnotemark[1]
The picture is complex so we will show it in three layers.
First are the arrows for nickels and pushing the dispense button.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa02.pdf}
\end{center}
After receiving $30$~cents and getting another nickel,
this machine does something not very sensible:~it stays in $q_{30}$.
In practice a machine would have further states to keep track of
overages so that we could give change, but here we ignore that.
Next comes the arrows for dimes
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa03.pdf}
\end{center}
and for quarters.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa04.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This machine, when started in state~$q_0$ and fed bit strings,
will keep track of the remainder modulo~$4$ of the number of
\str{1}'s.
\begin{center}    
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa06.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}\footnotetext[1]{%
  US coin denominations are: $1$~cent coins that are not used here,
  nickles are $5$~cents, dimes are $10$~cents, and quarters are $25$~cents.}

%<*def:FSM>
\begin{definition} \label{def:FiniteStateMachine}
A \definend{Finite State machine},\index{Finite State machine}
or
\definend{Finite State automata},\index{Finite State automata} 
is composed of five things
$\FSM=\sequence{Q,q_{\text{start}},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$.
They are a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,\index{state}\index{Finite State machine!state}
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{\text{start}}$,\index{start state}
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of 
\definend{accepting states}\index{accepting state}\index{state!accepting}\index{Finite State machine!accepting state} 
or
\definend{final states},\index{final state}\index{state!final}\index{Finite State machine!final state}
a finite \definend{input alphabet}\index{alphabet!input}\index{input alphabet}\index{Finite State machine!alphabet} 
set~$\Sigma$,
and a 
\definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function}\index{function!next-state}\index{Finite State machine!next-state function}
or \citetext{\definend{transition function} 
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{Q}$}{%
  Some authors allow the transition function to be partial.
  That is, some authors allow that for some state-symbol pairs
  there is no next state.
  This choice by an author is a matter of convenience, as for any such
  machine you can create a state $q_{\text{error}}$ that is not an 
  accepting state and that transitions only to itself, 
  and send all such pairs there.
  This transition function is total, and the new machine has the same
  collection of accepted strings as the old.}.\index{transition function}\index{function!transition}\index{Finite State machine!transition function}
\end{definition}
%</def:FSM>

This may not immediately appear to be like our
definition of a Turing Machine.
Some of that is because we have already defined the terms
`alphabet' and `transition function'.
The other differences follow from the fact that that
Finite State machines cannot write.
For one thing, because Finite State machines cannot write they don't need
to move the tape for scratch work,
so we've dropped the tape action symbols \str{L} and~\str{R}.

The other difference between Finite State machines and Turing machines
is the presence of the accepting states.
Consider, in the vending machine of \cref{ex:VendingMachine},
the state $q_{30}$.
It is an accepting state, meaning
that the machine has seen in the input what it is looking for.
The same goes for \cref{ex:Turnstile}'s turnstile state~$q_{\text{ready}}$
and \cref{ex:PowerSwitch}'s power switch state~$q_{\text{on}}$.
While we can design a Turing Machine to indicate a choice by arranging so
that for each input
the machine will halt and the only thing on the tape will be either a \str{1}
or \str{0},
a Finite State machine.
gives a decision by ending in one of these designated states.
Above we've pictured that the accepting states are connected to a red light
so that we know when a computation succeeds.
In the transition graphs below we denote the final states with double circles
and in the transition function tables we mark them with
a `\acceptingstatemark'.

To work a Finite State machine device, put the
finite-length input on the tape and press \texttt{Start}.\index{Start button@\texttt{Start} button}
The machine consumes the input, at each step reading in the next
tape character.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine02.pdf}
\end{center}
Consequently there is no \HP{} for Finite State machines\Dash they always halt
after a number of steps equal to the length of the input.
At the end either the \texttt{Accept} light is on or it isn't. 
If it is on then we say the machine 
\definend{accepts}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by deterministic Finite State machine} 
the input string, otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}\index{rejects}\index{string rejected}
the string.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}
This machine accepts a string if and only if it
contains at least two \str{0}'s as well as an even number of \str{1}'s.
(The $\acceptingstatemark$ next to $q_2$ marks it
as an accepting state.)
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa11.pdf}}               
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$    \\
    \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_5$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
    $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
    $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
This machine illustrates the key to designing Finite State machines, that each
state has an intuitive meaning.
The state~$q_4$ means ``so far the machine has seen one~$0$ and an
odd number of $1$'s.''
And $q_5$ means ``so far the machine has
seen two \str{0}'s but an odd number of \str{1}'s.''
The drawing brings out this principle.
Its first row has states that have so far seen an even number of
\str{1}'s, while the second row's states have seen an odd number.
Its first column holds states have seen no \str{0}'s,
the second column holds states have seen one,
and the third column has states that have seen two \str{0}'s.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAValidIntegers}
%<*ex:FSAValidIntegers>
This machine
accepts strings that are valid as decimal representations of integers.
Thus, it accepts `\str{21}' and `\str{-707}' but does not accept
`\str{501-}'.
Both the transition graph and the table group some inputs together when
they result in the same action.
For instance, when in state~$q_0$ this machine does the same thing
whether the input is \str{+} or \str{-}, namely it passes into
$q_1$.
%</ex:FSAValidIntegers>
\begin{center}  \small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa07.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{+}, \str{-} &\str{0}, \ldots{} \str{9} &\tabulartext{else}   \\
    \cline{2-4}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $q_1$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    \acceptingstate $q_2$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $e$   &$e$   &$e$   &$e$   \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
  Any bad input character sends the machine to the error state.~$e$,
  which is a sink state, meaning that the machine never leaves that state.
\end{example}

Often our Finite State machine descriptions will assume that a
reader can infer the alphabet from the context.
For instance, the prior example just says `else'. 
In practice we would take the alphabet to be the
set of characters that someone could conceivably enter, 
including letters such as \str{a} and~\str{A} or
characters such as exclamation point or open parenthesis.
Thus, design of a Finite State machine up to a modern standard would use 
all of \citetext{Unicode}{While in the early days of computers
  characters could be encoded with standards such as ASCII, which includes only
  upper and lower case unaccented letters, digits,
  a few punctuation marks, and a few
  control characters,
  today's global interconnected world needs more.
  The Unicode standard assigns a unique number called a code point to
  every character in every language (to a fair approximation).
  See \autocite{wiki:Unicode}.\index{Unicode}}.\index{Unicode}
But for the examples and exercises here that volume
is not relevant so we will use small alphabets.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}
This machine accepts strings that are members of the set
$\set{\str{jpg},\str{pdf},\str{png}}$ of filename extensions.
Notice that it has more than one final state.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa09.pdf}}
\end{center}
That drawing omits many edges, the ones involving the error state~$e$.
For instance, from state~$q_0$ any input character other than \str{j}
or~\str{p} is an error.
The drawing below shows the extra edges for the top branch; putting in all the
edges would make a mess.
Cases such as this are where the transition table is better
than the graph picture.
But most of our machines are small and have only a few states and edges
so we typically prefer the picture.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa10.pdf}}
\end{center}
This example illustrates that  
for any finite language there is a Finite State machine that accepts
a string if and only if it is a member of the language.
The idea is to put the strings in alphabetical order, and for ones that have
common prefixes have the machine step through the shared parts together,
as here with \str{pdf} and \str{png}.
\Cref{exer:FiniteLanguageAcceptedBySomeFSM} asks for a proof.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:FSAMultOfThree}
Although they have no scratch memory, Finite State machines can
accomplish useful work such as some kinds of arithmetic.
This machine accepts strings representing a natural number
that is a multiple of three, such as \str{15} and~\str{5013}.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa12.pdf}}
\end{center}
Because $q_0$ is an accepting state, this machine accepts the empty string.
\Cref{exer:FSAModifyMultOFThreeMachine} asks for a modification of
this machine to accept only non-empty strings.
\end{example}

Here is a Scheme version of the multiple of three machine.\footnotemark[1]

  One of the great things about the Scheme programming languages is that,

\begin{example}
This is a simplified version of 
\citetext{how phone numbers used to be handled in North America}{%
  See the description of the North America Numbering Plan
  \autocite{wiki:NANumbering}.}.
Consider the number \str{1-802-555-0101}.
The iniital \str{1} signifies that 
the call should leave the local exchange office to go to the long lines.
The \str{802} is an area code; the system can tell this is so because
its second digit is either~\str{0}
or~\str{1} so it is not a 
\citetext{same-area local exchange}{%
  Initially, large states, those divided into multiple numbering plan areas 
  were assigned area codes with a \str{1} in the second position 
  while areas that covered entire states or provinces got codes with 
  \str{0} as the middle digit. This was abandoned by the early 1950s.
  \autocite{wiki:NANumbering}.}.
Next the system processes the local exchange number of \str{555},
routing the call to a particular physical local switching office.
That office processes the line number of \str{0101}, and makes the connection.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa13.pdf}
\end{center}
Today the picture is much more complicated.
For example, no longer are area codes required to
have a middle digit of \str{0} or~\str{1}.
This additional complication is possible because instead of
\citetext{switching with physical devices}{%
  The devices to do the switching were invented in 1889 by an undertaker whose
  competiter's wife was the local telephone operator and routed calls to
  her husband's business.
  \autocite{wiki:Strowger}},
we now do it in software.
\end{example}

After the definition of Turing machine we gave a formal
description of the action of those machines.
We next do the same here.
% Finite State machines are easy to understand so this may seem 
% unnecessary but in addition to the principle that it is always
% good to have clearly stated rules,
% it pays off later when we consider the variant of nondeterministic
% Finite State machines, which are less easy to understand and
% so having rules and being able to contrast them with these rules
% is help.  

\label{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine}
%<*discussion:FSMConfiguration>
A 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{Finite State machine!configuration} 
of a Finite State machine is 
a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$, where
$q$ is a state, $q\in Q$, and $\tau$ is a (possibly empty) 
string,~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
We start a machine with some 
\definend{input}\index{input string}\index{Finite State machine!input string} 
string~$\tau_0$
and say that the 
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{Finite State machine!initial configuration}
is~$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$.

A Finite State machine acts by a sequence of \definend{transitions}    
from one configuration to another.
For $s\in\N^+$ the machine's configuration after the $s$-th transition 
is its configuration at 
\definend{step~$s$},\index{step}\index{Finite State machine!step} 
$\mathcal{C}_s$.

Here is the rule for making a transition
(we sometimes refer to this as an 
\definend{allowed} or \definend{legal} transition, for emphasis).
Suppose that the machine is in the configuration
$\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$.
In the case that $\tau_s$ is not empty, pop the string's leading symbol~$c$.
That is, where
$c=\tau_s[0]$, take 
$\tau_{s+1}=\sequence{\tau_s[1],\ldots \tau_s[k]}$ for
$k=\lh{\tau_s}-1$.
Then the machine's next state is $\hat{q}=\Delta(q,c)$ and its next
configuration is $\mathcal{C}_{s+1}=\sequence{\hat{q},\tau_{s+1}}$.
Denote this before-after relationship between configurations
by $\mathcal{C}_s\yields\mathcal{C}_{s+1}$.\footnote{%
  Read the symbol $\yields$ aloud as ``yields'' or ``yields in one step.''}

The other case is that the string~$\tau_s$ is empty.
This is the 
\definend{halting configuration}\index{halting configuration}\index{configuration!halting}\index{Finite State machine!halting confuguration} 
$\mathcal{C}_h$.
No transitions follow a halting configuration.

At each transition the length of the tape string drops by one so every 
computation eventually reaches a halting configuration
$\mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q,\emptystring}$.
A 
\definend{Finite State machine computation}\index{computation!Finite State machine}\index{Finite State machine!computation}
is a sequence 
$
  \mathcal{C}_0 \yields \mathcal{C}_1 \yields \mathcal{C}_2
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h
$.
We can abbreviate such a sequence
with~$\kleenestar{\yields}$,\footnote{%
  Read it as ``yields eventually,'' or simply ``yields.''}
as in
$\mathcal{C}_0\mathrel{\kleenestar{\yields}}\mathcal{C}_h$. 
If the ending state is a final state, $q\in F$,
then the machine \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by deterministic Finite State machine} 
the input~$\tau_0$,
otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{Finite State machine!reject string}
%</discussion:FSMConfiguration>

Notice that, as with the formalism for Turing machines, 
the heart of the definitions is the transition function~$\Delta$.
It makes the machine move step-by-step, from configuration to configuration,
in response to the input.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:MultipleOfThreeFormal}
The multiple of three machine of the prior example gives this computation.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{5013}}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,013}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,13}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,3}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
Since $q_0$ is an accepting state, the machine accepts \str{5013}.
\end{example}

%<*def:FSMLanguage>
\begin{definition}
The set of strings accepted by a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ is the
\definend{language accepted by that machine,~$\lang(\FSM)$}.
\end{definition}
%</def:FSMLanguage>
% We close with a definition that emphasizes the role played
% by determinism.

%<*def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>
\begin{definition} \label{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn}
  For any Finite State machine
  with transition
  function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$,
  the 
  \definend{extended transition function}\index{extended transition function}\index{transition function!extended}\index{function!extended transition} 
  $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{Q}$
  gives the state that the machine will end in after starting in the
  start state and consuming the given string.
\end{definition}
%</def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>

Here is a constructive definition.
Fix a Finite State machine $\FSM$ with
transition function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$.
To begin, set $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q_0}$.
Then for $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$, define
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau\concat t)=
  \Delta(q_{i_0},t)\cup\Delta(q_{i_1},t)\cup\cdots \cup\Delta(q_{i_k},t)$
for any $t\in\Sigma$. 
Finally, observe that a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is accepted 
by the machine if
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ is a final state.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:ExtendedTransFcn}
  This machine's extended transition function $\extended{\Delta}$
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa15.pdf}}
    \hspace{2em}
    \begin{transitionfunction}
             &\str{a}  &\str{b}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate
      $q_1$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
      $q_2$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
  extends its ordinary transition function $\Delta$ 
  in that it repeats the first row of $\Delta$'s table.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{a})=q_1
    \qquad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{b})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
  (We disregard the difference between characters and length one strings, 
  meaning that $\Delta$'s inputs are characters
  while $\extended{\Delta}$'s input are strings, and above that means length
  one strings.)
  Here is $\extended{\Delta}$'s effect on the length~two strings.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{aa})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ab})=q_2
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ba})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{bb})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
\end{example}

The definition of the extended transition function brings us back to thinking
about determinism.
That's because $\extended{\Delta}$ would not be well-defined
without determinism; $\Delta$ has one and only one next state
for all input configurations and so, by induction, for all input strings
$\extended{\Delta}$ has one and only one output ending state.

Finally, note the similarity between $\extended{\Delta}$
and $\phi_e$, the function computed by the Turing machine $\TM_e$.
Both take as input the contents of their machine's start tape,
and both give as output their machine's result.

\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
True or false:~because a Finite State is finite, its language must be
finite.
\begin{answer}\verified
  False.
  An example is that \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}'s machine accepts the
  set containing each of these: \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{2}, \ldots
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your classmate says, ``I have a language~$\lang$ that accepts
the empty string~$\emptystring$.''
Strighten them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  A language can contain the empty string.
  A machine can accept the empty string.
  But we have no definition for a language accepting a string.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
How many transitions does an input string of length~$n$ cause 
a Finite State machine to undergo?  
$n$?  $n+1$?  $n-1$?
How many (not necessarily distinct) states will the machine have visited
after consuming the string?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each input character consumed causes one transition.
  So an input string of length~$n$ causes the machine to undergo~$n$
  transitions.
  (This includes that the empty string~\emptystring causes no transitions.)
  With~$n$ transitions, the machine will have visited~$n+1$ many
  not necessarily distinct states, including the start state.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Rebut ``no Finite State machine can accept the language
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$
because $n$ is infinite.''
\begin{answer}\verified
  First, ``$n$ is infinite'' is wrong.
  That set is a language $\set{\str{b}, \str{ab}, \str{a}^2\str{b}, \ldots}$
  and in each string from that language the number of \str{a}'s is finite.

  Second, this Finite State machine accepts the language.
  \begin{center}  \small
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa31.pdf}}
  \end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each of these formal descriptions of a language, give a 
one or two sentence English-language description.
Also list 
five strings that are elements as well as five that are not,
if there are five. 
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang=\set{\alpha\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\alpha=\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$
\item $\set{\beta\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\beta=\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^m$ for $n,m\in\N$}}$
\item $\set{\str{b}\str{a}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               n\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^{n+2}\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
            \suchthat n\in\N}$
\item $\set{\gamma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\gamma$ has the form $\gamma=\alpha\concat\alpha$
                     for $\alpha\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item A string is in this language if and only if it
    has some number of \str{a}'s, followed by a single \str{b},
    followed by the same number of \str{a}'s.
    That is, the two strings of \str{a}'s have the same length.
    Five elements of the language are 
    \str{aba}, \str{aabaa}, \str{b}, \str{aaabaaa}, and \str{aaaabaaaa}.
    Five non-elements are \str{ba}, \str{aaba}, \str{a}, \str{abba},
    and~\str{bbabb}.
  \item The strings in this language have some number of \str{a}'s followed
    by a single~\str{b}, followed by a number of~\str{a}'s.
    The two numbers of~\str{a}'s need not be the same, and may be zero.
    Five language elements are \str{abaa}, \str{aabaa}, \str{ab}, \str{baaaa},
    and~\str{b}.
    Five non-elements are \str{bab}, \str{aabb}, \str{bb}, \str{aabaab}, 
    and~\str{a}.
  \item The criteria for a string to be in this language is that it must
    start with~\str{b} and be followed by some number of~\str{a}'s.
    That could be zero-many \str{a}'s.
    Five elements of the language are \str{baa}, \str{ba}, \str{b},
    $\str{b}\str{a}^3$, and~$\str{b}\str{a}^4$.
    Five non-elements are \str{a}, \emptystring, \str{abaaa}, \str{baaab},
    and~\str{bb}. 
  \item A string is in this language if and only if it consists of some number
    of \str{a}'s followed by a~\str{b}, followed by some number of~\str{a}'s,
    where the second string of~\str{a}'s has two more than the first string.
    Five members of the language are \str{abaaa}, \str{aabaaaa},
    $\str{a}^3\str{b}\str{a}^5$, $\str{a}^4\str{b}\str{a}^6$, and~\str{baa}.
    Five non-members are \str{aba}, \str{abaaaa}, \str{ba}, \str{b},
    and~\str{bab}.
  \item This language has members that are doubles in that a member consists
    of the concatenation of two copies of some string.
    Five members of the language are \str{aaabaaab}, \str{babbab}, \str{aa},
    \str{bbbbabbbba}, and~\str{abbaabba}.
    Five non-members are \str{aba}, \str{b}, \str{aaabaab}, \str{babaa}, 
    and~\str{bbb}. 
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % Models of Computation, Taylor p 509
For the machines of \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}, \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}, 
\cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}, and \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree},
answer these.
\begin{exparts*}
\item What are the accepting states?  
\item Does it accept the empty string~$\emptystring$?
\item What is the shortest string that each accepts?
\item Is the language of accepted strings infinite?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
    For~\cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} the only accepting state is $q_2$.
    In~\cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} also, the only accepting state is $q_2$.
    For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} the accepting states 
    are $q_3$, $q_6$, and $q_8$.
    For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is $q_0$.
  \item
    Only \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} accepts the empty string~$\emptystring$ 
    because only in this machine is $q_0$ an accepting state.
  \item
    For \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} the shortest accepted string 
    is the length two string~\str{00}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} it is any single-digit length one string,
    such as \str{9}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} any of the length three strings
    \str{jpg}, \str{png}, and~\str{pdf}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is the empty string~$\emptystring$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce the transition graph picturing this transition
function.
What is the language of this machine?
\begin{center}
  \small
  \begin{transitionfunction}
           &\str{a} &\str{b}    \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$q_2$   &$q_1$       \\
    \acceptingstate $q_1$  &$q_0$   &$q_2$       \\
    $q_2$  &$q_2$   &$q_2$           
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  This is the graph picture.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa40.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The language is
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
         \text{$\sigma=\str{b}(\str{ab})^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

What language is accepted by this machine?

  The language is the set of strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a Finite State machine
over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$ that accepts
any string containing the substring \str{abc}.
As in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs},
give a brief description of each state's 
intuitive meaning in the machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
  To accept strings containing the substring \str{abc} the machine 
  must have a state whose intuitive meaning is ``have just seen~\str{a},
  next looking for \str{bc}.''
  That state is~$q_1$.
  The state $q_2$ means that the machine has just processed a 
  substring~\str{ab} and is looking for~\str{c}.
  And $q_3$ means the machine has seen the substring~\str{abc}.
  Finally, $q_0$ is the state the machine is in if it has so far
  not seen even the first character in a potential substring~\str{abc}.
  Here is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa29.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Note that if the machine processes~\str{ab} followed by~\str{b}
  then it returns to~$q_0$, but if it processes~\str{ab} followed by~\str{a}
  then it does not return to~$q_0$, it passes to~$q_1$.
  This follows from the intuitive meaning of~$q_1$.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the language of strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ containing
at least two \str{a}'s and at least two \str{b}'s.
Name five elements of the language, and five nonelements.
Give a Finite State machine accepting this language.
As in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}, give a brief description of the 
intuitive meaning of each state.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Five elements of the language are 
  \str{aabb}, \str{bbaa}, \str{abab}, \str{aababb}, and~$\str{a}^3\str{b}^4$.
  Five nonelements are 
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aab}, and~\str{bbbbabbbb}.
  Here is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa30.pdf}}
  \end{center}

  The intuitive meaning of the states in the first row,
  $q_0$, $q_1$, and~$q_2$, is that 
  when the machine is in those states it has so far seen zero-many \str{b}'s.
  States in the second row are where the machine has so far seen
  one~\str{b}.
  The third row contains the states where the machine has seen two \str{b}'s.
  Similarly, the first column contains the states where the machine 
  has so far seen no \str{a}'s, in the second column are states 
  where the machine
  has seen a single~\str{a}, and in the final column the machine has
  see two \str{a}'s. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each language, name five strings in the language and five that are not 
(if there are not five, name as many as there are).
Then give a transition graph and table
for a Finite State machine accepting the language.
Use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has exactly two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has less than three \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{a} followed by at least one \str{b}}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
  Five strings that are elements of the language are 
  \str{aa}, \str{aaa}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, and~\str{baa}.
  Five that are not are 
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{ab}, and~\str{ba}.
  This machine machine accepts only strings in that language.
  \begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa23.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item Five elements of the language are
  \str{aa}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, \str{baa}, and~\str{aabb}.
  Five that are not in the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ba}, \str{aaa}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa24.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item
  Five strings that are elements of the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, and~\str{ab}.
  Five that are not are
  \str{aaa}, \str{aaab}, \str{aaba}, \str{abaa}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \begin{center}
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa25.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
 \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item
  Five from the language are
  \str{ab}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, \str{abb}, and~\str{bab}.
  Five that are not in the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{ba}, and~\str{bbbbaaaa}.
  \begin{center}
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa26.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Finite State machine over the alphabet
$\Sigma=\set{\str{A},\,\ldots \str{Z},\str{0},\ldots \str{9}}$ 
that accepts only the
string \str{911},
and a machine that accepts any string but that one.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first machine accepts only \str{911} while the second

\begin{exercise}
What happens when the input to an extended transition function
is the empty string?
\begin{answer}\verified
  It outputs the length~one string containing the start state,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=q_0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider this grammar.   
\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{real} \produces{} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{+} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{-} \ntrm{posreal}

\ntrm{posreal} \produces{} \ntrm{natural} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{natural} \produces{} \ntrm{digit} \syntaxor \ntrm{digit} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{digit} \produces{} \trm{0} \syntaxor \ldots{}  \trm{9}
\end{grammar}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give five strings that are in its language and five that are not.
\item Is the string \str{.12} in the language?
\item Briefly describe the language.
\item Give a Finite State machine that accepts the language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Five that are: \str{+1}, \str{123}, \str{-123.45}, \str{-0.0},
  and~\str{0}.
  Five that are not: \str{+}, \str{+-}, \str{..}, \str{12.3.4}, and~\str{2+3}
  \item No, before the period must come at least one digit.
  \item It is the set of representations of real numbers with integer part,
  a decimal point, and a decimal part.
  \item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa41.pdf}}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language produce five strings from that language and five that are
not, and also 
produce a Finite State machine to accept it.
\begin{exparts}
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{every \str{1} in $\sigma$ is both immediately preceded and followed by a \str{0}}}$
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is the binary representation of a number divisible by $4$}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is the decimal representation of a number that is even}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a number divisible by $100$ in base ten}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Five that are in the language are \str{010}, \str{0010}, \str{0}, 
    \str{0101000}, and~\emptystring.
    Five that are not are \str{10}, \str{0110}, \str{0101}, \str{01001},
    and~\str{1}.
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa42.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item 
    A string is a binary representation of a multiple of~$4$ if and only
    if it ends in two zero's, \str{00}, or it is~\str{0}.
    Five in the language are \str{100}, \str{0100}, \str{1000}, \str{10100},
    and~\str{0}.
    Five not in the language are \str{10}, \str{1}, \str{1001}, \str{001},
    and~\emptystring. 
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa43.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item Five strings in the language are \str{0}, \str{20}, \str{12}, \str{88},
    and~\str{08}.
    Five that are not are \str{1}, \str{01}, \str{2221}, \str{17}, 
    and~\emptystring.
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa44.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item This is basically the same as the second item.
    A string is a decimal representation of a multiple of~$100$ if and only
    if it ends in two zero's, \str{00}, or it is~\str{0}.
    Five in the language are \str{100}, \str{0100}, \str{2000}, \str{60300},
    and~\str{0}.
    Five not in the language are \str{10}, \str{8}, \str{1009}, \str{001},
    and~\emptystring. 
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa45.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$
   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
     \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a multiple of $4$ in base ten}}
$.
Briefly describe a Finite State machine;
you need not give the full graph or table.
\begin{answer}\verified
  A number is divisible by four if and only if its representation ends in
  \str{00}, \str{04}, \ldots \str{96}, or the single-digit cases where the
  representation is \str{0}, \str{4},
  or~\str{8}.
  That's $28$ cases.
  A picture would be cluttered but this is a finite language
  and for any finite language there is a Finite State machines that
  accepts that language.
  (We shall see an alternative approach that makes a clearer
  picture in the section on nondeterminism.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As in \cref{ex:MultipleOfThreeFormal}, find the computation for
the multiple of three machine with the initial string
\str{2332}.
\begin{answer}
This is the computation.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{2332}}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,332}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,32}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,2}
  \yields\sequence{1_0,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
The state $q_1$ is not an accepting state
so the machine rejects \str{2332}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  We will through the formal definition of the extended transition function
  that follows \cref{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn} by 
  applying it to the machine in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$
    and $\extended{\Delta}(\str{1})$.
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}$'s output on inputs
    \str{00}, \str{01}, \str{10}, and \str{11}.
  \item Find its action on all length~three strings.
  \end{exparts}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Recall the transition function for that example.
    \begin{center}
      \begin{transitionfunction}
        &\str{0}    &\str{1}   \\
        \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
      $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$    \\
      \acceptingstate
      $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_5$  \\
      $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
      $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
      $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
    \end{center}
    \begin{exparts}
    \item If the input string is $\sigma=\str{0}$ then peel off the final
      character with
      $\sigma=\sigma_0\concat\str{0}=\emptystring\concat\str{0}$ 
      and the definition gives this.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{0})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_0,\str{0})
        =q_1
      \end{equation*}
      (Note that in the left-most expression \str{0} is a length one string,
      while in the rest of the above equation \str{0} is a character,
      and we are ignoring this uninteresting point.)
      Similarly, $\extended{\Delta}(\str{1})=q_3$.
    \item For $\sigma=\str{00}$ use the prior item's computation of
      $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{00})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{0}),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_1,\str{0})
        =q_2
      \end{equation*}
      The other three are similar.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{01})=q_4
        \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{10})=q_4
        \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{11})=q_0
      \end{equation*}
    \item
      For each of these use the prior item's computation.
      Here is the first.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{000})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{00}),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_2,\str{0})
        =q_2
      \end{equation*}
      The others are computed in the same way.
      \begin{center}
      \begin{tabular}{*{8}{c}}
        $\extended{\Delta}(\str{000})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{001})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{010})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{011})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{100})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{101})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{110})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{111})$  \\ \hline
        $q_2$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_3$
      \end{tabular}
      \end{center}
    \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  From \textit{Introduction to Languages} by Martin, edition four, p~77.
\end{credit}
Produce a Finite State machine that accepts the language over
$\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
containing no more than one occurrence of the substring~\str{aa}.
That is, it may contain zero-many such substrings or one, but not two.
Note that the string \str{aaa} contains two occurrences of that
substring.
  \begin{answer}
    \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa48.pdf}}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $\Sigma=\B$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
List all of the different
Finite State machines over $\Sigma$ with a single state, $Q=\set{q_0}$.
(Ignore whether a state is final or not; we will do that below.)
\item
List all the the ones with two states, $Q=\set{q_0,q_1}$.
\item
How many machines are there with~$n$ states?
\item
What if we distinguish between machines with different sets of final states?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  There is one such machine.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \item
  This list has $16$ machines.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_0]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_1]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_1$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_2]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_0$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}\raisebox{-1ex}{\ldots}\hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{15}]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \item
  Fix $n$ states, $Q=\set{q_0,\ldots q_{n-1}}$.
  Each line of the transition table has two columns so the number of
  distinct lines is $n\cdot n=n^2$.
  An overall table chooses $n$~such lines.
  There are $n^2\cdot n^2 \cdots n^2=n^{2n}$ many ways to do that.
  \item
  For each of the $n$~states, either it is final or it is not.
  The additional factor of $2^n$ makes the total number of distinct tables
  equal to~$2^nn^{2n}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\textit{Propositiones ad acuendos iuvenes} (problems to sharpen
the young) is the oldest collection
of mathematical problems written in Latin.
It teaches logical thinking and problem solving and was 
written by 
\citetext{Alcuin of York (735--804)}{%
  See \url{https://www.bbc.co.uk/programmes/m000dqy8}.}, royal advisor
to Charlemagne
and head of the Frankish court school at Aachen.
One problem,
\textit{Propositio de lupo et capra etfasciculo cauli}, is particularly famous:
\textit{A man had to transport to the far side of a river
\citetext{a wolf, a goat, and a bundle of cabbages}{%  Annie Raymond
  This translation is from A~Raymond, from the University of Washington.}.
The only boat he could find was one that could
carry only two of them. For that reason, he sought a plan which would
enable them all to get to the far side unhurt. Let him, who is able, say
how it could be possible to transport them safely.}
Readers would have understood that a wolf cannot be left
alone with a goat nor can a goat be left alone with cabbages.
Construct the relevant Finite State machine and use it to solve the problem.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We can have the man, the wolf, the goat, and the cabbage,
  on the near or far side.
  The allowed transitions are: man crossses with wolf,
  man crosses with goat, man crosses with cabbage, and man crosses by himself.
  Here are the states; some of them lead to an error state, as readers of the
  $700$'s would have understood.

  In the Finite State machine shown in \figureref{fig:WolfGoatCabbage}
  the start state is $s_0$ and the only accepting state is~$s_{15}$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{cc|cccc}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{state}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{meaning}}
                              &\str{with wolf}
                              &\str{with goat}
                              &\str{with cabbage}
                              &\str{with nothing}  \\  
      \hline   
       $s_0$  &n:~mwgc, f:~-- &$s_{12}$ &$s_{10}$  &$s_9$    &$s_8$      \\
       $s_1$  &n:~mwg, f:~c    &$s_{13}$ &$s_{11}$  &$e$      &$s_9$   \\
       $s_2$  &n:~mwc, f:~g    &$s_{14}$ &$e$       &$s_{11}$ &$s_{10}$     \\
       $s_3$  &n:~mw, f:~gc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
       $s_4$  &n:~mgc, f:~w    &$e$      &$s_{14}$  &$s_{13}$ &$s_{12}$      \\
       $s_5$  &n:~mg, f:~wc    &$e$      &$s_{15}$  &$e$      &$s_{13}$      \\
       $s_6$  &n:~mc, f:~wg    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
       $s_7$  &n:~m, f:~wgc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$       \\
       $s_8$  &n:~wgc, f:~m--  &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
       $s_9$  &n:~wg, f:~mc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
       $s_{10}$  &n:~wc, f:~mg &$e$      &$s_0$     &$e$     &$s_2$       \\
       $s_{11}$  &n:~w, f:~mgc &$e$      &$s_1$     &$s_{10}$ &$s_3$        \\
       $s_{12}$  &n:~gc, f:~mw &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
       $s_{13}$  &n:~g, f:~mwc &$s_1$    &$e$       &$s_4$    &$s_5$         \\
       $s_{14}$  &n:~c, f:~mwg &$s_2$    &$s_4$     &$e$      &$s_6$         \\
       $s_{15}$  &n:~--, f:~mwgc  &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$\\
       $e$    &error           &$e$     &$e$       &$e$      &$e$   \\
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Finite state machine for Wolf-Goat-Cabbage.}
    \label{fig:WolfGoatCabbage}
  \end{figure}
  In words:
  \textit{The man first brings the goat to the far side and leaves it there.
  He goes back and brings the wolf with him, leaving it on the far side,
  and brings back the goat.
  He leaves the goat on the initial side, takes the cabbage and brings it
  to the far side.
  Finally, he goes back to the original shore, and takes the goat to bring
  it to the far side.}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider a variant on a Finite State machine, where the set of input
strings is bounded.
\begin{exparts}
\item
In Rock-Paper-Scissors,
two players each simultaneously
produce one of the three \str{R}, \str{P}, or~\str{S}.  
A player producing \str{R} beats a player producing~\str{S},
and similarly \str{S} beats~\str{P}, and \str{P} beats~\str{R}
(if they produce the same then it is a do-over).
Encode a game instance with the two-character string
$\sigma=\sequence{\text{player one's play, player two's play}}$.
Make a machine that recognizes a win for player one.
\item
Make a machine that accepts a Tic-Tac-Toe game that is a win for
the \str{X}'s.
A board has nine squares so encode a game instance with length nine strings.
\end{exparts}
Both the examples illustrate particularly well how the machines
use their states as a kind of memory to keep track of
what has happened in the compuation so far.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  There are three ways for the first player to win.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa36.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  \item
  Encode the ending position as a length nine string.
  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
    $a_{0,0}$ &$a_{0,1}$ &$a_{0,2}$  \\ \hline
    $a_{1,0}$ &$a_{1,1}$ &$a_{1,2}$  \\ \hline
    $a_{2,0}$ &$a_{2,1}$ &$a_{2,2}$  \\ 
  \end{tabular}
  \hspace{1em}$\Longrightarrow$\hspace{1em}$a_{0,0}a_{1,0}\ldots{} a_{2,2}$
  \qquad $a_{i,j}\in\Sigma=\set{\str{X},\str{E}}$
  \end{center}
  Here, \str{E} means `else'\Dash either  player~\str{O}
  marked that square or it is blank 
  because the game ended before that square was filled.
  There are eight ways for \str{X} to win.
  \begin{align*}
  &\begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
  \end{array},\hspace{1em}
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
  \end{array},\hspace{1em}
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{X} &\str{X} &\str{X} \\  
    \end{array},                                \\
  &\quad
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
  \end{array},\hspace{1em}
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\  
  \end{array},\hspace{1em}
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\  
  \end{array},                              \\  
  &\quad
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\  
  \end{array},\hspace{1em}
  \begin{array}{c|c|c}
    \str{E} &\str{E} &\str{X} \\ \hline
    \str{E} &\str{X} &\str{E} \\ \hline
    \str{X} &\str{E} &\str{E} \\  
  \end{array}
  \end{align*}
  The machine takes the shape of a binary tree of depth nine.
  It has $2^9$ branches and $2^{10}$ total state nodes, too many to
  show here so this picture just shows ellipses.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa49.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Put double-circle states for the terminal node in each of the eight
  branches described above, such as
  \str{XXXEEEEEE}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Can a Finite State machine have no states;
% does the definition allow $Q=\emptyset$?  
% \begin{answer}
%  No, a Finite State machine cannot have $Q=\emptyset$.
%  The definition requires a start state.
