% \begin{exercise}
% Show that any language accepted by a Finite State machine is
% accepted by some Turing machine.
% (There are lots of ways to define `accepted by a Turing machine'.
% One is that the Turing machine
% reads the input and halts with the head under the only symbol
% on the tape, a \str{1}.)
% \begin{answer}
%   Fix some Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
%   We shall  outline a computer program that acts as this machine.
%   By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine equivalent.

%   The program has as a constant the Finite State machine's
%   transition function~$\Delta$,
%   perhaps simply storing the transition table as an array.
%   The program uses this to simulate the Finite State machine.
%   As input this program takes the input of the Finite State machine.

%   This simulation halts because a Finite State machine must halt.
%   When it does so, the Turing machine erases the tape.
%   If the simulation ended in an accepting state then
%   the Turing machine writes a \str{1}, otherwise it writes a~\str{0}.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Fix a Finite State machine $\FSM$, its set of accepting states~$F$,
% and the associated language~$\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
% of accepted strings.
% If we flip every accepting state to non-accepting and every non-accepting
% state to accepting, what happens to the language?
% \begin{answer}
%  If we take the new set of final states to be $\hat{F}=Q-F$ then the
%  machine will accept the complementary language
%  $\hat{\lang}=\kleenestar{\Sigma}-\lang$.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:FiniteLanguageAcceptedBySomeFSM}
Show, as suggested by
\cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}, that for any finite language,
there is a Finite State machine that accepts a string if and only if it
is a member of that language.
\begin{answer}
  This is based on \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}.

  We will prove this by induction on the number of strings in the language.
  If the language is empty then we use any
  Finite State machine with the right alphabet and no accepting states.
  If the language has one string~$\sigma$ then we write a Finite State machine
  whose state transitions track the characters of~$\sigma$ as does the top line
  in the example.
  That is, we have this.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa46.pdf}
  \end{center}
  As the diagram shows, the last state in that sequence is an accepting state.
  Also as shown, all transitions for characters not in the sequence go to $e$.
  (If $\sigma=\emptystring$ then there are two states,
  with the initial state~$q_0$ an accepting
  state and all transitions going to the error state~$e$ that is not an accepting
  state.)

  For the inductive case,
  assume that there is a machine for any language of size $n=1$, \ldots $n=k$
  and consider a language~$\lang$ with $k+1$-many strings.
  Order the alphabet $\Sigma$; in the example
  we use the grade school alphabetical ordering.
  This character ordering gives rise to an ordering of the strings,
  called lexicographic ordering.
  List $\lang$'s strings lexicographically:~$\sigma_0$, \ldots{} $\sigma_k$, 
  $\sigma_{k+1}$.

  The induction assumption gives a machine for the language
  consisting of the strings $\sigma_0$, \ldots{} $\sigma_k$.
  The example shows what to do next; it has the two final strings
  \str{png} and \str{pdf} and the machine splits where the two strings split,
  after the~\str{p}.
  Following that model, 
  find the longest common substring for $\sigma_k$ and~$\sigma_{k+1}$,
  so that $\sigma_k=\alpha\concat\beta$, and $\sigma_{k+1}=\alpha\concat\gamma$,
  and the initial character of $\beta$ is not equal to the initial character
  of $\gamma$.
  (It could be that $\beta=\emptystring$.
  It could also be that $\alpha=\emptystring$.)
  As in the example, split the machine after it has traversed the common string.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa47.pdf}
  \end{center}
  (To be more readable this figure omits all the transitions involving~$e$.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  We can use cardinality to show that there are languages such that 
  no Finite State machine accepts that language. 
  Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$ with at least two members.
  \begin{exparts*}
    \item Show that the number of Finite State machines with that 
       alphabet is infinite.
    \item Show that it is countable.
    \item Show that the number of languages over that alphabet is
      uncountable.
  \end{exparts*} 
\begin{answer}\verified
  Recall that an alphabet is finite, by definition.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  We will show that there are infinitely many
  different Finite State machines.
  Fix any $\Sigma$ and suppose $\str{s}\in\Sigma$.
  The machines below differ in the number of states,
  and in that they accept different languages.
  (If $\Sigma$ contains characters other than~\str{s}
  then use the second column.)
  \begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other} \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other} \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$ \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_2$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}\ldots  
  \end{center}
  That shows, under a reasonable interpretation of what it means for
  machines to differ, that there are at least countably many Finite State
  machines.
  (The technical wording is that these machines are not isomorphic.)
  \item 
  The argument that there are no more than countably many machines also
  requires an interpretation.
  Assume that the set of possible states is countable,
  $Q=\set{q_0,q_1,\ldots}$, so as to not get uncountably many
  machines by virtue of there being uncountably many possible states.
  With that, the argument that we can count the
  Finite State machines is straightforward since for any collection of finitely
  many states $q_0,\ldots q_k$ there are finitely many transition tables.
  Thus the number of Finite State machines using those states is countable.
  And then the union of countably many countable sets is countable,
  so the number of machines over all collections of finitely many states
  is countable.
  \item Because $\Sigma$ has at least two members, the usual 
   diagonalization construction shows that the power set of $\Sigma$
   is not countable. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{Finite State machine|)}







% ==================================================
\section{Nondeterminism}\index{nondeterminism|(}
Turing machines and Finite State machines both have the property that
the next state is completely determined by the current state and current
character.
Once you lay out an initial tape and push \texttt{Start} then
you just walk through the steps like, well \dots, like an automaton.
We now consider machines that are nondeterministic, 
where from any configuration the machine could move to more than
one next state, or to just one, or even to no state at all.


\subsection{Motivation}
Suppose that you have a grammar
with some rules and start symbol~$S$.
You are given the string~$\sigma$ and asked if has a derivation.
The challenge to these problems is that
you sometimes have to guess which path the derivation should take.
For instance, if you have
\mbox{S\produces{}\trm{a}S\syntaxor\trm{b}A} then
from S you can do two different things;
which one will work? 

In the grammar section's derivation exercises, we expect that an 
intelligent person 
have the insight to guess the right way. 
However, 
if instead you were solving this problem with a program then you might 
have it try every
possible case;~you might do a breadth-first traversal of the tree of all
derivations until you found a success.

% Although the breadth-first technique is familiar from experience with
% programming classes, don't think that it is a natural fit. 
% This problem is most naturally done in parallel and breadth-first traversal is
% a serialization that we do only because it is required by
% the single-CPU device model that we use when we first learn programming.

\margingraphic{0.175\textwidth}{automata/pix/yogi.jpg}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogi_Berra}{Yogi Berra 1925--2015}}\index{Berra, Y!picture}
The American philosopher and Hall of Fame baseball catcher Y~Berra
said, ``When you come to a fork in the road, take it.''
That's a natural way to attack this problem:~when you come up against
multiple possibilities,
fork a child for each.
Thus, the routine might begin with the start state~$S$ and for each rule that
could apply it spawns a child process, deriving a
string one removed from the start.
After that, each child finds each rule that could apply to its string and
spawns its own children, each of which now has a string that is two removed from
the start.
Continue until the desired string~$\sigma$ appears, if it ever does.

The prototypical example is 
the celebrated 
\citetext{Travelling Salesman problem}{%
  See 
  \url{https://nbviewer.jupyter.org/url/norvig.com/ipython/TSP.ipynb}.},\index{Travelling Salesman problem@\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem}
that of finding the shortest circuit of every city in a list.
Start with a map of the 
\citetext{roads in the US lower forty eight states}{
  See \url{https://wiki.openstreetmap.org/wiki/TIGER}.}.
We want to know if there is a trip that visits each
state capital and returns back to where it began in, say, 
less than $16\,000$ kilometers.
We'll start at Montpelier, the capital of Vermont.
From there, for each potential next capital we could fork a process,
making forty seven new processes.
The process that is assigned Concord, New Hampshire, for instance, 
would know that the trip so far is $188$~kilometers. 
In the next round, each child would fork its own child processes, 
forty six of them.
For instance the process that after Montpelier was assigned Concord
would have a child assigned to Augusta, Maine and would know that
so far the trip is $452$~kilometers.
At the end, if there is
a trip of less than $16\,000$ kilometers then some process knows it.
There will be lots of processes and many of them will have failed
to find a short trip, but if even one succeeds then we consider
the overall search a success.

\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{automata/pix/angel.jpg}{\label{fig:Angel}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel}{Persian angel, 1555}} % Kneeling Angel from Bukhara School c. 1555-1560. British Museum https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Persian_angel_1555.jpg [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
This computation is nondeterministic in that while it is happening
the machine has many process, and so
is simultaneously in many different states.
it perfectly suits an unboundedly-parallel machine, one where any time
you have a job for an additional computing agent, a CPU, you can allocate 
one.\footnote{%
  Alternatively, a single CPU can simulate the children with time-slicing,
  dovetailing by running each process in succession for a few ticks.
  This echos our experience with everyday computers, where
  the machine appears to be
  in multiple states simultaneously 
  when we are writing an email in one window 
  and watching a video in another,
  although it may in fact be alternating between jobs.}
Think of such a machine as
angelic in that whenever it wants more computational resources, 
such as being able to allocate new children, those resources just appear.

This section considers nondeterministic Finite State machines.
(Nondeterministic Turing machines appear in the fifth chapter.)
We will have two ways to think about nondeterminism,
two mental models.\footnote{%
  While these models are helpful in learning and thinking about nondeterminism,
  they are not part of the formal definitions and proofs.}
The first was introduced above:~we
can picture that when such a mathine 
is presented with multiple possible next states then
the machine forks, so that it is in all of them simultaneously.
The next example illustrates.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}
The Finite State machine below is nondeterministic because 
leaving $q_0$ are two arrows labelled~\str{0}.
It also has states with a deficit of edges; e.g.,
no~\str{1} arrow leaves $q_1$, so if it is in that state and reads that
input then it passes to no state at all.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
\end{center}
The animation shows what happens with input \str{00001}. 
We take the computation history as a tree.
For example, on the first \str{0} the computation splits in two.
\begin{animatedfigure}{Steps in the nondeterministic computation.} 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{3}{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm}{02}{08}
  \label{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree}
\end{animatedfigure}
% \begin{center}
%   \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm08.pdf}
% \end{center}
\end{example}
When we considered the forking approach to string derivations or to
the Travelling Salesman,
we observed that if a solution exists then some child process would find it.
The same happens here; there is a
branch of the computation tree that accepts the input string.
There are also branches that are not successful.
The one at the bottom dies out after step~$2$
because when the present
state~$q_2$ and the input is~\str{0} this machine passes to 
\citetext{no-state}{%
  A person can wonder about no-state.
  Where is it, exactly?
  We can thing that it is like what happens if you write 
  a program with a sequence of if-then statements and forget to include an else.
  Obviously a computer goes somewhere, the instruction pointer points to
  some next address, but what happens is not sensible in terms of
  the model you mean.

  Alternatively, the wonderful book \autocite{Hofstadter} describes a place 
  named Tumbolia, which is where holes go when they are filled
  (also where your lap goes when you stand).
  Perhaps the machines go there.}.\footnote{%
  No-state cannot be an accepting state, since it isn't a state at all.
}
Another is the branch at the top, which never dies but
also does not accept the input.
However we don't care about unsuccessful branches,
we only care that there is a successful one.
So we will define that a nondeterministic machine accepts an input
if there is at least one branch in the computation tree that accepts the
input.

The machine in the above example
accepts a string if it ends in two~\str{0}'s and a~\str{1}.
When we feed it the input \str{00001} the problem the machine faces 
is:~when it should stop going around $q_0$'s loop and start to the right?
Our definition has the machine accepting this input
so the machine has solved this problem\Dash viewed from the outside
we could say, perhaps a bit fancifully, that the
machine has correctly guessed.

This is our second model for nondeterminism.
We will imagine programming by calling a function, some 
\citetext{$\operatorname{amb}(S,R_0,R_1\ldots R_{n-1})$}{%
  The name \textit{amb}\index{amb@\textit{amb} function}
  abbreviates `ambiguous function'.
  Here is a small example.
  Essentially \str{Amb(x,y,z)} splits the computation
  into three possible futures:
  a future in which the value \str{x} is yielded,
  a future in which the value \str{y} is yielded,
  and a future in which the value \str{z} is yielded.
  The future which leads to a successful subsequent computation is chosen.
  The other ``parallel universes'' somehow go away.
  (Amb called with no arguments fails.)
% \begin{lstlistings}
% (let ((x (amb 1 2 3))
%       (y (amb 5 4 3)))
%   (amb (= (* x y) 8)
%   (println x y)))
% \end{lstlistings}
  The output is $2$~$4$ because \str{Amb(1,2,3)} correctly chooses the future
  in which \str{x} has value $2$, \str{Amb(7,6,4,5)} chooses $4$,
  and consequently
  \str{Amb(x*y = 8)} produces a success.
  
  These were described by John McCarthy in \autocite{McCarthy}.
  ``Ambiguous functions are not really functions.
  For each prescription of values
  to the arguments the ambiguous function has a collection of possible values.
  An example of an ambiguous function is
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$ defined for all positive integer
  values of $n$.
  Every non-negative integer less than $n$ is a possible value of
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$.
  First we define a basic ambiguity operator $\operatorname{amb}(x, y)$
  whose possible
  values are $x$ and~$y$ when both are defined: otherwise, whichever is defined.
  Now we can define $\operatorname{less}(n)$ by
  $\operatorname{less}(n) = \operatorname{amb}(n-1, \operatorname{less}(n-1))$.''},
and having the computer somehow guess a successful search sequence.\index{guessing by a machine}

\margingraphic{0.22\textwidth}{automata/pix/flauros.jpg}{\label{fig:Flauros}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_demons_in_the_Ars_Goetia}{The demon Flauros, Duke of Hell}}\index{Flauros!picture} %, gives true answers of all things past, present, and future}} % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AIll_dict_infernal_p0294-278_flauros_demon.jpg Louis Le Breton [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
% \margingraphic{0.25\textwidth}{automata/demon.jpg}{\label{fig:Demon}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nightmare}{\textit{The Nightmare} H~Fuseli, 1781}} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/56/John_Henry_Fuseli_-_The_Nightmare.JPG  Henry Fuseli [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons  (https://www.atlasobscura.com/articles/original-nightmare-demon-suffocation-night-terror)
Saying that the machine is guessing is jarring.
%  since  
% \citetext{it does not fit our intuition of how computers work}{%
%   \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Aq_1l316ow8}{``That's not how this works, that's not how any of this works''}}.
% But researchers in this area often frame it in this way.
A less disconcerting way to put it is to imagine that the machine is
furnished with the answer (``go around twice, then off to the right'') and
only has to check it.
This mental model of nondeterminism is demonic because
the furnisher seems to be a supernatural being, 
\citetext{a demon}{%
  The term `demon' comes from Maxwell's demon.
  This is a a thought experiment created in 1867 by the physicist J~C Maxwell,
  about the second law of thermodynamics, which says that it takes energy
  to raise the temperature of a sealed system.
  Maxwell imagined a chamber of gas with a door controlled
  by an all-knowing demon.
  When the demon sees a gas molecule of gas approaching that is slow-moving, 
  it opens the door and lets it out of the chamber, 
  thereby raising the chamber's temperature
  without applying any external heat.
  See \autocite{wiki:MaxwellsDemon}.},\index{demon, or daemon}
who somehow knows answers that cannot otherwise be found,
but you are suspicious and must check that the answer is not a trick.
Under this model, a nondeterministic computation
accepts the input if there exists a branch of the computation tree
that a deterministic machine, if told what branch to take, could verify.

Below we shall describe nondeterminism using both paradigms:~as a
machine being in multiple states at once, and as a machine guessing.
As mentioned above, here we will do that for Finite State
machines but in the fifth chapter we will return to it in the
context of Turing machines. 
% To use both paradigms we must reconcile them by showing
% that there is a nondeterministic machine accepting
% a string under the first model if and only if there is one accepting
% that string under the second.
% Here we will do that for nondeterministic
% Finite State machines.




\subsection{Definition}

A nondeterministic Finite State machine
differs from a deterministic machine only in that
the next-state function does not output single states,
it outputs sets of states.

%<*def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine}
A 
\definend{nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{NFSM|see {nondeterministic Finite State machine}}\index{Finite State machine!nondeterministic}\index{nondeterminism!for Finite State machines}
$\FSM=\sequence{Q,q_{\text{start}},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$ 
consists of a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{\text{start}}$,
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of \definend{accepting states}\index{accepting state!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
or \definend{final states},\index{final state!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
a finite \definend{input alphabet} set~$\Sigma$,
and a \definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset (Q)}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>

We will use these machines in three ways.
First, with them we encounter
nondeterminism, which is critical for the book's final part. 
Second, they are useful in practice;
for some examples of jobs that are more easily solved in this way
see the exercises.
Finally, we use them to prove Kleene's Theorem,
\cref{th:KleenesTheorem} below.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NDFMFromDiscussion}
This is \cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}'s nondeterministic Finite State machine,
along with its transition function.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}             \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $q_1$  &$\set{q_2}$      &$\set{}$     \\
    $q_2$  &$\set{}$         &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    \acceptingstate{} $q_3$  &$\set{}$     &$\set{}$   \\    
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
In this nondeterministic machine
the entries of the array are not states, they are sets of states.
\end{example}

Nondeterministic machines are  
less conceptually clear than deterministic ones
so the formalities are a big help here;
contrast this with the definitions on 
page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine}.

A 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!configuration} 
is a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$, where
$q\in Q$ and $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
A machine starts with some 
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!initial configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$,
using the \definend{input}\index{input string}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!input string} 
string~$\tau_0$.

Following an initial configuration there may be one or more sequences of 
\definend{transitions}.
Suppose that there is a machine configuration
$\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$.
For $s\in\N^+$, in the case where $\tau_s$ is not the empty string, 
a
transition pops the string's leading symbol~$c$ to get~$\tau_{s+1}$, 
takes the machine's next state to be a member~$\hat{q}$ of the 
set~$\Delta(q,c)$ and 
then takes a subsequent configuration to be 
$\mathcal{C}_{s+1}=\sequence{\hat{q},\tau_{s+1}}$.
Denote that two configurations are connected by a transition
with $\mathcal{C}_s\yields\mathcal{C}_{s+1}$.

The other case is that~$\tau_s$ is the empty string.
This is a 
\definend{halting configuration}\index{halting configuration},\index{configuration!halting}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!halting confuguration} 
$\mathcal{C}_h$.
After~$\mathcal{C}_h$, no transitions follow.

% The computation may eventually reache a halting configuration
% $\mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q,\emptystring}$.
A 
\definend{nondeterministic Finite State machine computation}\index{computation!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation}
is a sequence of transitions that ends in a halting configuration,
$
  \mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0} 
   \yields \mathcal{C}_1 \yields \mathcal{C}_2
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q,\emptystring}
$.
% Abbreviate this
% with
% $\mathcal{C}_0\mathrel{\kleenestar{\yields}}\mathcal{C}_h$. 
From an initial configuration  
there may be many such sequences.
If at least one ends with a halting state, with $q\in F$,
then the machine \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
the input~$\tau_0$,
otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!reject string}

\begin{example}
For the nondeterminstic machine of \cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}, the graphic
shows this allowed sequence of transitions.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{00001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{0001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{01}}     \\
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{1}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\emptystring}    
\end{equation*}
Because it ends in an accepting state, the machine accepts the
initial string,~\str{00001}.
\end{example}

%<*def:NFSMAccepted}
\begin{definition}\label{def:NFSMAccepted}
For a nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$ 
the 
\definend{language of the machine~$\lang(\FSM)$}\index{language!of a nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!language of} 
is the set of accepted strings.
\end{definition}
%</def:NFSMAccepted>

\label{discussion:NFSMStringAcceptance}
We will also adapt the definition 
of $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{Q}$, the 
\definend{extended transition function}\index{extended transition function!nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
here, to accommodate the sets of states.
Fix a nondeterministic~$\FSM$ with
transition function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$.
Start with $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q_0}$.
Then, where
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\ldots{} q_{i_k}}$
for $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$, define
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau\concat t)=
  \Delta(q_{i_0},t)\cup\Delta(q_{i_1},t)\cup\cdots \cup\Delta(q_{i_k},t)$
for any $t\in\Sigma$. 
With that, observe that
$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ is accepted if any element of
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ is a final state.

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}
The language accepted by this nondeterministic machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm09.pdf}
\end{center}
is the set of strings containing the substring \str{aa} or \str{bb}.
For instance, the machine accepts \str{abaaba} 
% where the 
% matching substring starts at index~$2$, 
because there is a
sequence of allowed transitions ending in an accepting state,
namely this one.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{abaaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{baaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{aaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{aba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{ba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{a}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}
With $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$,
this nondeterministic machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm10.pdf}
\end{center}
accepts the language $\set{(\str{ac})^n\suchthat n\in\N}
  =\set{\emptystring,\str{ac},\str{acac},\,\ldots\,}$.
The symbol~\str{b} isn't attached to any arrow
so it won't play a part in any accepting string.
\end{example}

One advantage of nondeterministic Finite State machines is that
they are often easier to write than a deterministic machine that 
does the same job.

\begin{example}
This nondeterministic machine that accepts any string
whose next to last character is \str{a}
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm62.pdf}
\end{center}
is simpler than the deterministic machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm88.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This machine accepts
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma=0\concat\tau\concat 1$ where $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm63.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}
This is a
garage door opener listener, that waits to hear the remote control send
the signal \str{0101110}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm11.pdf}
\end{center}
That is, it accepts the language
$\set{\sigma\concat\str{0101110}\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$
of strings with that suffix.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
With a couple of examples under our belt, before going on we pause
to again acknowledge that something about nondeterminism
is unsettling.
If we feed the prior example's listener the string
$\tau=\str{010101110}$ then it accepts.
After all, this is a sequence of allowed transitions.
\begin{multline*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{010101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{10101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{0101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{101110}}     \\
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{01110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{1110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_4,\str{110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_5,\str{10}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_6,\str{0}}
  \yields \sequence{q_7,\emptystring}
\end{multline*}
But~$\tau$ begins with three sets of \str{01}'s 
while the machine's chain of states
is set up for a string, \str{0101110}, that begins with two.
How can a mechanism guess that it should ignore
the first~\str{01} but act on the second?
Based on an introduction to programming class,
a person's intuition may be that ``guessing'' is a
not-mechanically-accomplishable task.\index{guessing by a machine}
Now, in mathematics
we can consider whatever we can define precisely. 
However, in this book we have so far emphasized
devices that are physically realizable at least in principle
so this may appear to be a change in direction. 

But it is not a change. 
We will show how to convert any nondeterministic Finite State
machine into deterministic one that does the same job.
So we can think of a nondeterministic machine
as an abbreviation, a convenience.
This obviates at least some of the paradox of guessing, 
at least for Finite State machines.
\end{remark}



\subsection{$\varepsilon$ transitions}\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions|(}
%<*discussion:epsilonTransitions>
Another extension, beyond nondeterminism, is to allow 
\definend{$\varepsilon$~transitions},\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machines!epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions} 
also known as 
\definend{$\varepsilon$ moves}.\index{epsilon moves@$\varepsilon$ moves}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machines!epsilon moves@$\varepsilon$ moves}
We alter 
the definition of a nondeterministic Finite State machine,
\cref{def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine}, 
so that instead of $\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset(Q)}$,
the transition function's signature is
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times(\Sigma\union\set{\emptystring})}{\powerset(Q)}$.\footnote{%
  We assume $\varepsilon\notin\Sigma$}
The associated behavior
is that the machine can transition spontaneously, 
without consuming any input.\footnote{%
  Or you  could think of it as transitioning on 
  consuming the empty string~$\emptystring$.}
%</discussion:epsilonTransitions>

\begin{example}
This machine accepts valid integer representations.
Note the $\varepsilon$ on the edge between $q_0$ and~$q_1$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm12.pdf}
\end{center}
Because of the $\varepsilon$
it can accept strings that do not start with a \str{+} or \str{-}~sign.
For instance, with input~\str{123} the machine 
can begin by following the $\varepsilon$
transition to state~$q_1$, then read the \str{1} and transition
to $q_2$, and stay there while processing the \str{2} and \str{3}.
This is a branch of the computation tree accepting the input,
and so the string \str{123} is in the machine's language.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:TwoOrMoreEpsilonTrans}
A machine may follow two or more $\varepsilon$ transitions in a row.
If this machine is in state~$q_0$ then it may stay in that state, or
transition to $q_{2}$, or $q_3$, or~$q_5$,
without consuming any input.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm83.pdf}
\end{center}
That is, the language of this machine is the four element set
$\lang=\set{\str{abc},\str{abd},\str{ac}, \str{ad}}$.
\end{example}

We can give a precise definition of the action of 
a nondeterministic Finite State machine with $\varepsilon$~transitions. 

First we define the collection of states reachable by $\varepsilon$~moves
from a given state.
For that we use
$\map{E}{Q\times\N}{\powerset (Q)}$ where $E(q,i)$ is
the set of states reachable from~$q$ within at most $i$-many 
$\varepsilon$~transitions.
That is, set $E(q,0)=\set{q}$ and where 
$E(q,i)=\set{q_{i_0},\ldots q_{i_k}}$,
set $E(q,i+1)=E(q,i)\cup \Delta(q_{i_0},\varepsilon)\cup
                         \cdots\cup\Delta(q_{i_k},\varepsilon)$.
Observe that these are nested,
$E(q,0)\subseteq E(q,1)\subseteq \cdots\,$ and that each is
a subset of~$Q$.
But~$Q$ has only finitely many states so there must be an $\hat{i}\in\N$
where the sequence of sets stops growing,
$E(q,\hat{i}\,)= E(q,\hat{i}+1)= \cdots\,$.
Define the \definend{$\varepsilon$~closure} function 
$\map{\hat{E}}{Q}{\powerset(Q)}$ by~$\hat{E}(q)=E(q,\hat{i}\,)$.

With that, we are ready to describe the machine's action.
As before, a 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!configuration} 
is a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$, where
$q\in Q$ and $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
A machine starts with some 
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!initial configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$, where the string~$\tau_0$.
is the \definend{input}.\index{input string}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!input string} 

The key description is that of a
\definend{transition}.
Consider a configuration
$\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$ for $s\in\N$ and suppose that
$\tau_s$ is not the empty string.
We will describe a configuration 
$\mathcal{C}_{s+1}=\sequence{\hat{q},\tau_{s+1}}$ that is related to the
given one by
$\mathcal{C}_s\yields\mathcal{C}_{s+1}$.
(As with the earlier description of nondeterministic machines without
$\varepsilon$~transitions, there may be more than one configuration
related in this way to $\mathcal{C}_s$.)

The string is easy; just pop the leading character 
to get $\tau_s=t\concat\tau_{s+1}$ where $t\in\Sigma$.
To get a legal state~$\hat{q}$:
(i)~find the $\varepsilon$~closure $\hat{E}(q)=\set{q_{s_0},\ldots q_{s_k}}$,
(ii)~let $\bar{q}$ be an element of the set
$\Delta(q_{s_0},t)\cup\Delta(q_{s_1},t)\cup\cdots\cup\Delta(q_{s_k},t)$,
and (iii)~take $\hat{q}$ to be an element of the $\varepsilon$~closure
$\hat{E}(\bar{q})$.

If $\tau_s$ is the empty string then 
this is a 
\definend{halting configuration}\index{halting configuration},\index{configuration!halting}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!halting confuguration} 
$\mathcal{C}_h$.
No transitions follow~$\mathcal{C}_h$.

A 
\definend{nondeterministic Finite State machine computation}\index{computation!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation}
is a sequence of transitions ending in a halting configuration,
$
  \mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0} 
   \yields \mathcal{C}_1 \yields \mathcal{C}_2
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q,\emptystring}
$.
From a given~$\mathcal{C}_0$ there may be many such sequences.
If at least one ends with a halting state, having $q\in F$,
then the machine \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
the input~$\tau_0$,
otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!reject string}

With that,
we will modify the definition of the extended transition 
function~$\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{Q}$\index{extended transition function!for nondeterministic Finite State machines}\index{transition function!extended} 
from \cpageref{discussion:NFSMStringAcceptance}.
Begin by defining
$\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\hat{E}(q_0)$.
Then the rule for going from a string to its extension is that 
for $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and 
where $\extended{\Delta}(\tau)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\ldots{} q_{i_k}}$. 
\begin{equation*}
  \extended{\Delta}(\tau\concat t)=
  \hat{E}(\Delta(q_{i_0},t))\cup\cdots \cup\hat{E}(\Delta(q_{i_k},t))
  \qquad
  \text{for $t\in\Sigma$}
  % \tag{*}
\end{equation*}
Observe that this nondeterministic machine with $\varepsilon$~transitions
accepts\index{accept an input}\index{Finite State machine!accept string}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ 
if any one of the states
in $\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ is a final state.

\begin{remark}
Certainly these are an intricate set of definitions.
However, they demonstrate something.
Although in the examples and the homework we often
use informal terms such as ``guess'' and ``demon,''
nonetheless we have not left mathematical orthodoxy.
If asked, we can 
perfectly well pin these ideas down with full mathematical precision.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
For the machine of \cref{ex:TwoOrMoreEpsilonTrans}, this sequence
shows it accepts~\str{abc}
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{abc}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{bc}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{c}}
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{c}}
  \yields \sequence{q_4,\emptystring}     
\end{equation*}
(note the $\varepsilon$~transition between $q_2$ and~$q_3$).
This sequence shows it also accepts the input string~\str{d}.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{d}}
  \yields \sequence{q_5,\str{d}}
  \yields \sequence{q_6,\emptystring}     
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

As with nondeterminstic machines, 
one reason that we use $\varepsilon$~transitions is that
they can make solving a complex job much easier.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMJoinParallel}
An $\varepsilon$ transition can put two machines together with a
parallel connection.
This shows a machine whose states are named with $q$'s combined with
one whose states are named with~$r$'s.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm14.pdf}
\end{center}
The top nondeterministic machine's language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{ab}}}$
and the bottom machine's language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for some $n\in\N$}}$,
where $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.
The language for the entire machine is the union.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \text{either $\sigma$ ends in \str{ab} or $\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for $n\in\N$}}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}
An $\varepsilon$ transition can also make a serial connection 
between machines.
The machine on the left below accepts the language
$\set{(\str{aab})^m\suchthat m\in\N}$
and the machine on the right accepts
$\set{(\str{a}|\str{aba})^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\begin{center} % align graphics on top https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/126919/121234
  % Get width of first graphic
  \newsavebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}
  \savebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm89.pdf}}
  \newlength{\jhtmplengthautomatai}
  \settowidth{\jhtmplengthautomatai}{\usebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}}
  % Get width of second graphic
  \newsavebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}
  \savebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm90.pdf}}
  \newlength{\jhtmplengthautomataii}
  \settowidth{\jhtmplengthautomataii}{\usebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}}
  % Tabulars align paragraphs on top
  \begin{tabular}{p{\jhtmplengthautomatai} @{\hspace*{3em}} p{\jhtmplengthautomatai}}
    \vspace{0pt} \usebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}
    &\vspace{0pt} \usebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
If we insert an $\varepsilon$~bridge from
each of the left side's final states 
(in this example there happens to be only one such state) 
to the right side's initial state 
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm13.pdf}
\end{center}
then the 
combined machine accepts strings in the concatenation of those languages.
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{L}(\FSM)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma=(\str{aab})^m(\str{a}|\str{aba})^n$ for $m,n\in\N$}}
\end{equation*}
For example, it accepts \str{aabaababa} and
\str{aabaabaaa}.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}
An $\varepsilon$ transition edge can also produce the Kleene star of a  
a nondeterministic machine.
For instance, without the $\varepsilon$~edge this machine's language is
$\set{\emptystring, \str{ab}}$,
while with it the language is  
$\set{(\str{ab})^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm15.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}
\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions|)}




% ........................................
\subsection{Equivalence of the machine types}\label{sec:EquivDFSMAndNFSM}
We next prove that
nondeterminism does not change what you can do
with Finite State machines. 

%<*th:dfsmEquivndfsm>
\begin{theorem}
  The class of languages accepted by nondeterministic Finite State machines
  equals the class of languages accepted by deterministic Finite State
  machines.
  This remains true if we allow the nondeterministic machines to
  have $\varepsilon$~transitions.
\end{theorem}
%</th:dfsmEquivndfsm>

We can show that the two classes are equal by showing that they are subsets of
each other.
One direction is easy;~any deterministic machine is,
basically, a nondeterministic machine.
That is, in a deterministic machine the next-state function outputs
single states and
to make it a nondeterministic machine just convert those states
into singleton sets.
Thus the set of languages accepted by
deterministic machines is a subset of the set accepted by
nondeterministic machines.

We will demonstrate inclusion in the other direction constructively.
We will show how to start
with a nondeterministic machine with~$\varepsilon$ transitions and
construct a deterministic machine that accepts the same language.
We won't give a complete proof (although
certainly one is possible) simply because
a proof is messy and the examples below
are entirely convincing.

% First consider a nondeterministic machine $\FSM_N$ 
% Let its next-state function be~$\Delta_N$.
% Produce
% the associated deterministic machine $\FSM_D$ by listing the
% sets of states of $\FSM_N$.
% The example below illustrates.
% Each line of the table is a set $s_i=\set{q_{i_1},q_{i_2}, \,\ldots}$
% of $\FSM_N$'s states.
% The next-state function $\Delta_D$ of $\FSM_D$  combines the
% next states associated with each $q_{i_j}$.
% \begin{equation*}
%   \Delta_D(s_i)=
%     \Delta_N(q_{i_1})\cup\Delta_N(q_{i_2})\cup \cdots
%   \tag*{($*$)}
% \end{equation*}

\begin{example}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!convert to a deterministic machine}
Consider this nondeterministic machine, $\FSM_N$,
with no $\varepsilon$ transitions.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm16.pdf}
\end{center}
What differentiates a nondeterministic machine is that it can be in
multiple states at once.
So for
the associated deterministic machine $\FSM_D$,
each line of the transition function's table below is a 
set $s_i=\set{q_{i_1}, \ldots{}\, q_{i_k}}$
of $\FSM_N$'s states.

As an illustration of constructing the table,
suppose that the above machine is in $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$ and is reading~\str{a}.
Combine the next states due to~$q_0$, the set
$\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})=\set{q_0,q_1}$, with the next states
due to~$q_2$, the set $\Delta_N(q_2,\str{a})=\set{}$.
The union of those two sets 
gives $\Delta_D(s_5,\str{a})=\set{q_0,q_1}$, which  
below is the state~$s_4$.
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
      &\str{a}   &\str{b}         \\
      \cline{2-3}
    $s_0=\set{}$                           &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_1=\set{q_0}$         &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    $s_2=\set{q_1}$                         &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$         &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_4=\set{q_0,q_1}$     &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$     &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$      &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
The start state of $\FSM_D$ is $s_1=\set{q_0}$.
A state of the deterministic machine $\FSM_D$ is accepting
if any of its element $q$'s are accepting states in $\FSM_N$.

In general, compute the transition function of the
deterministic machine with
$\Delta_D(s_i,x)
  =\Delta_N(q_{i_0},x)\cup\cdots \cup\Delta_N(q_{i_k},x)$, 
where $s_i=\set{q_{i_0}, \ldots{}\, q_{i_k}}$ and $x\in\Sigma$.
An example is 
$\Delta_D(s_5,\str{a})=\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})\cup\Delta_N(q_2,\str{a})$,
which equals $\set{q_0,q_1}\cup\set{}=\set{q_0,q_1}=s_4$.

Besides the notational convenience,
naming the sets of states as $s_i$'s makes clear that
$\FSM_D$ is a deterministic Finite State machine.
So does showing its transition graph.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm17.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

If the nondeterministic machine has~$k$-many states then
under this construction the deterministic machine has $2^k$-many states.
Typically many of them can be eliminated.
For instance, in the above machine
the state~$s_6$ is clearly unreachable 
since there are no arrows into it.
The next section covers minimizing the number of states in a machine.

We next expand that construction to cover~$\varepsilon$ transitions.
Basically, we follow those transitions.
For example, the start state of the deterministic machine
is the $\varepsilon$~closure of~$\set{q_0}$, 
the set of the states of~$\FSM_N$ that
are reachable by a sequence of $\varepsilon$~transitions from $q_0$.
In addition, suppose that we have a nondeterministic machine and 
we are constructing the associated deterministic machine's
next-state function~$\Delta_D$,
that the current configuration is $s_i=\set{q_{i_1},q_{i_2}, \,\ldots}$ and
that the machine is reading \str{a}.
If there is a $\varepsilon$ transition from $q_{i_j}$ to some~$q$ then
to the set of next states add $\Delta_N(\set{q},\str{a})$, and in fact add
the entire $\varepsilon$~closure.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure}
Consider this nondeterministic machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm18.pdf}
\end{center}
To find the set of next states, follow the 
$\varepsilon$~transitions.
For instance, suppose that this machine is in~$q_0$ and the next tape character 
is~\str{a}.
The arrow on the left takes the machine
from $q_0$ to~$q_2$.
Alternatively,
following the $\varepsilon$ transition from $q_0$ to~$q_3$
and then reading the~\str{a} gives~$q_3$.
So the machine is next in the two states $q_0$ and~$q_3$.
These are the $\varepsilon$~closures.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}}
  \tabulartext{state}~$q$ 
     &$q_0$           &$q_1$          &$q_2$        &$q_3$  
  \\ \cline{2-5}
  \tabulartext{$\varepsilon$ closure $\hat{E}(q)$}
     &$\set{q_0,q_3}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1}$ &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_3}$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

This is the full deterministic machine. 
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
      &\str{a}   &\str{b}         \\
      \cline{2-3}
    $s_0=\set{}$                                 &$s_0$     &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_1=\set{q_0}$              &$s_{10}$  &$s_2$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_2=\set{q_1}$              &$s_{10}$  &$s_2$  \\
                    $s_3=\set{q_2}$              &$s_0$     &$s_7$  \\
                    $s_4=\set{q_3}$              &$s_4$     &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_0,q_2}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_8=\set{q_1,q_2}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_9=\set{q_1,q_3}$          &$s_{10}$  &$s_{2}$  \\
                    $s_{10}=\set{q_2,q_3}$          &$s_4$    &$s_7$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{11}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{12}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{13}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{14}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_{15}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$  &$s_{10}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
The start state is the $\varepsilon$~closure of $\set{q_0}$,
the state $s_7=\set{q_0, q_3}$.
A state is accepting if it contains any element of the 
$\varepsilon$ closure of~$q_1$.

In general, for a state~$s$ and tape character~$x$,
to compute $\Delta_D(s,x)$: 
(i)~find the $\varepsilon$~closure of all of the $q$'s in the state~$s$, 
  giving $\cup_{q\in s}\hat{E}(q)=\set{q_{i_0},\ldots q_{i_k}}$,
then (ii)~take the union of the next states of the $q_{i_j}$ to get
     $T=\Delta_N(q_{i_0},x)\cup \ldots \cup\Delta_N(q_{i_0},x)$
and finally (iii)~find the $\varepsilon$~closure of each element of~$T$, 
  and take the union of all those,
  $\cup_{t\in T}\hat{E}(t)$.

As an example take $s=s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$ and $x=\str{a}$.
Then~(i) gives $\set{q_0,q_3}\cup\set{q_0,q_1}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$.
The next states for~(ii) are 
$\Delta_N(q_0,\str{a})=\set{q_2,q_3}$, 
$\Delta_N(q_1,\str{a})=\set{q_2,q_3}$, 
and
$\Delta_N(q_3,\str{a})=\set{q_3}$,
so $T=\set{q_2,q_3}$.
Finally, for~(iii) the union of the $\varepsilon$~closures gives
$\set{q_2}\cup\set{q_3}=\set{q_2,q_3}=s_{10}$. 
\end{example} 




% .....................................
\begin{exercises}
  % \hspace*{0.7em}\textit{Unless specified otherwise, you can use
  %   $\varepsilon$ transitions in the nondeterministic machines
  %   in these exercises.}
\begin{exercise}
Give the transition function for the machine of
\cref{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}, and of \cref{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The main point is that entries in the table are sets of states.
  This is the machine in \cref{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                               &\str{a}           &\str{b}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
                      $q_0$    &$\set{q_0,q_{1}}$ &$\set{q_0,q_2}$   \\
                      $q_1$    &$\set{q_3}$       &$\set{}$   \\
                      $q_2$    &$\set{}$          &$\set{q_3}$   \\
      \acceptingstate $q_3$    &$\set{q_3}$       &$\set{q_3}$   \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  This is \cref{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}'s machine.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                               &\str{a}       &\str{b}   &\str{c}      \\
      \cline{2-4}
      \acceptingstate $q_0$    &$\set{q_{1}}$ &$\set{}$  &$\set{}$     \\
                      $q_1$    &$\set{}$      &$\set{}$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Consider this machine.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm30.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item Does it accept the empty string?
  \item The string \str{0}?
  \item \str{011}?
  \item \str{010}?
  % \item Are all strings of the form $\str{1}^j$ accepted, for $j>0$?
  \item List all length five accepted strings.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Yes.
  From $q_0$ a $\varepsilon$~transition goes to $q_2$, which is an accepting
  state.
  \item No.
  From $q_0$ the machine can either read the~\str{0} and go to
  $q_1$, or take the $\varepsilon$~transition
  to $q_2$ and then read the~\str{0}, which sends the machine to 
  no-state.
  Neither $q_2$ nor no-state is an accepting state.

  Said more formally, the $\varepsilon$~closure of $q_0$ is $\set{q_0,q_2}$.
  Neither state, on reading the character~\str{0}, transitions to a state
  whose $\varepsilon$~closure includes an accepting state. 
  \item Yes.
  With that input the machine can go from $q_0$ to~$q_1$, then to~$q_2$,
  and then around the loop to~$q_2$ again.
  More formally, this is a sequence of allowed transitions that ends in an
  accepting state.
  \begin{equation*}
    \sequence{q_0,\str{011}}
    \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{11}}
    \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{1}}
    \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
  \end{equation*}
  \item No.
  Starting at~$q_0$ with the character~\str{0} first on the tape the machine
  can do two things.
  If it takes the $\varepsilon$~transition then when it reads the~\str{0}
  it goes to no-state, which is not an accepting state.

  Otherwise, the~\str{0} moves the machine to~$q_1$.
  Next, on the following tape character~\str{1}, 
  the machine moves to~$\set{q_2}$.
  Finally, on the third character~\str{0},
  the machine moves to no-state. 
  \item There are two: \str{01111} and~\str{11111}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Your friend objects, ``Epsilon transitions don't make any sense
  because the machine below will never get its first
  step done; it just endlessly follows the epsilons.''
  Correct their mistake.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm59.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    The machine accepts the input string  
    if there is a sequence of legal transitions 
    that allows the machine to process 
    the string and that ends in an accepting state.
    It does not say there must not be any wrong ways to go, only
    that there must be a right way.

    For instance, the given machine 
    can accept \str{aab} by starting in $q_0$,
    transitioning to $q_1$ on the first \str{a},
    taking the $\varepsilon$ transition to~$q_0$,
    then going to $q_1$ on the second~\str{a}, 
    then going to $q_2$, which is accepting, with the~\str{b}.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{q_0,\str{aab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
    \end{equation*}
    This machine accepts the language
    $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:MFSMWithEpsilonTrans}
This machine has $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm19.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Does it accept the empty string?
\item \str{a}?
  \str{b}?  
\item Show that it accepts \str{aab} by producing a suitable sequence
  of $\yields$ relations.
\item List five strings of minimal length that it accepts.
\item List five of minimal length that it does not accept.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item It does not acccept the empty string because the $\varepsilon$~closure
  of $q_0$ does not contain any final states, since the $\varepsilon$~closure
  equals $\set{q_0}$.
  \item It accepts both of the one-character strings \str{a} and~\str{b}.
  For instance, on the string~\str{a} the machine can transition 
  from $q_0$ to~$q_1$,
  and then it can make an $\varepsilon$~transition to~$q_2$, which is 
  an accepting state.
  \item The given machine 
    can accept \str{aab} by starting in $q_0$,
    transitioning to $q_0$ on the first \str{a},
    then going to $q_1$ on the second~\str{a}, 
    taking the $\varepsilon$ transition to~$q_2$,
    then going to $q_2$ with the~\str{b}, thereby ending in an
    accepting state.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{q_0,\str{aab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
    \end{equation*}
  \item Five of the shortest strings are: \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, \str{ab},
  and~\str{aab}.
  \item Five that it does not accept are:~the empty string~$\emptystring$,
  \str{ba}, \str{baa}, \str{bab}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Produce the table description of the next-state function $\Delta$
  for the machine in the prior exercise.
  It should have three columns, for \str{a}, \str{b}, and $\varepsilon$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                             &$\varepsilon$ &\str{a}        &\str{b} \\
                             \cline{2-4}
                       $q_0$ &$\set{q_1}$   &$\set{q_{1}}$  &$\set{}$   \\
                       $q_1$ &$\set{q_0}$   &$\set{}$       &$\set{q_2}$  \\
       \acceptingstate $q_2$ &$\set{}$      &$\set{}$       &$\set{}$  
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % Taylor p 511
This table
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
            &\str{a}      &\str{b}         \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$   &$\set{q_0}$  &$\set{q_1,q_2}$  \\
    $q_1$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    $q_2$   &$\set{q_1}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $q_3$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
gives the next-state function for a nondeterministic Finite State machine.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Draw the transition graph.
\item What is the accepted language?
\item Give the next-state table for a deterministic machine that accepts the
  same language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \
  \begin{display}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1000.pdf}}
  \end{display}
  \item A string is in the language if it consists of, first, 
  some number of repetitions of:~either a number of
  repetitions of~\str{a} or \str{ba}.
  Then it has either \str{bb}, or a~\str{b} followed by an \str{a} or~\str{b}.
  Finally, the suffix consists of some number of characters, either \str{a}
  or~\str{b}. 
  \item This is the transition function for the associated deterministic 
  machine. 
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
                                                      &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
                      \cline{2-3}
                      $s_0=\set{}$                    &$s_0$    &$s_0$  \\
                      $s_1=\set{q_0}$                 &$s_1$    &$s_8$  \\
                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$                 &$s_2$    &$s_4$  \\
                      $s_4=\set{q_3}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\
                      $s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$             &$s_7$    &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_0,q_2}$             &$s_5$    &$s_{14}$  \\
                      $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$             &$s_7$    &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_8=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_9$    &$s_4$  \\
                      $s_9=\set{q_1,q_3}$             &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{10}=\set{q_2,q_3}$          &$s_9$     &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{11}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$      &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
                      $s_{12}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$      &$s_7$     &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{13}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{14}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_9$     &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{15}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$  &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Find the nondeterministic Finite State machine that accepts all bitstrings
  that begin with \str{10}.
  Use the algorithm given above to produce the transition function table
  of a deterministic machine that does the same.
  \begin{answer}\verified
    \begin{display}
      \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1004.pdf}
    \end{display}
    The associated deterministic machine has eight states. 
    Its accepting states are the ones containing~$q_2$.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
                                                      &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\
                      \cline{2-3}
                      $s_0=\set{}$                    &$s_0$    &$s_0$  \\
                      $s_1=\set{q_0}$                 &$s_0$    &$s_2$  \\
                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_3$    &$s_0$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$                 &$s_3$    &$s_3$  \\
                      $s_4=\set{q_0, q_1}$            &$s_3$    &$s_2$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$             &$s_3$    &$s_{6}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_3$    &$s_{3}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$         &$s_3$    &$s_{6}$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

                      $s_1=\set{q_0}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_1$  \\
                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_3$    &$s_3$  \\

      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_3$    &$s_{5}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$         &$s_7$    &$s_{5}$  \\
