% =================================================
\section{Regular languages}\index{regular language|(}\index{language!regular|(}

%...................................
\subsection{Definition}
Finite State machines are interesting because, at least, they give us insight
into what a machine can do without scratch memory.
We can get an idea of how powerful such a machine is by determining
the set of jobs that they can and cannot do.

%<*def:RegularLangs>
\begin{definition}
  A \definend{regular language}\index{regular language}\index{language!regular} 
  is one that is 
  accepted by some Finite State machine.
\end{definition}
%</def:RegularLangs>

There are infinitely many regular languages.
For one thing, every finite language $\lang_0=\set{}$,
$\lang_1=\set{\str{0}}$, $\lang_2=\set{\str{00}}$, \ldots{}
is regular; just list all the cases as in \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}.

If the language is finite but large then constructing the machine
by listing cases may not be practical.
For example,
there are finitely many people in the world and each has finitely many
active phone numbers so strictly speaking
the set of all currently-active phone numbers
is a regular language but constructing a Finite State machine for it
is 
\urldef\goldbachurl\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldbach%27s_conjecture}%
silly.\footnote{A finite 
  regular language doesn't have to be large for it to be
  difficult, in some sense.
  For example, consider
  \citetext{\definend{Goldbach's conjecture}}{See \goldbachurl.}, that
  every even number greater
  than~$2$ is the sum of
  two primes, as in $4=2+2$, $6=3+3$, $8=3+5$, \ldots\sentencespace
  Computer testing shows that this pattern continues to hold, up to very large
  numbers, but no one knows if it is true for all evens.
  Now consider the set consisting of
  the string~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots\,\str{9}}}$
  representing the smallest even number
  that is not the sum of two primes.
  This set is finite since it has either
  one member or none.
  But while that set is tiny, we don't know what it contains.}

\subsection{Closure properties}
In the proof of Kleene's Theorem we saw that if two languages
are regular then their union is also regular.
We have also seen, in \cref{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial},
that if two languages are regular then their concatenation
is regular.
We say that 
\citetext{a structure is \definend{closed} under an operation if
performing that operation on its members always yields another member}{%
  Familiar examples are that adding two integers always gives an integer 
  so the integers are closed under the operation of addition, and 
  that squaring an integer always results in an integer
  so that the integers are closed under squaring.}.\index{closed under an operation}

%<*lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>
\begin{lemma}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under
  union, concatenation, and Kleene star.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:RegLangsClosedUnderUnionConcatStar>

\begin{proof}
  In proving \cref{lem:KleenesThmRegexToMachine},
  the first half of Kleene's Theorem,
  we did the regular expression operations of pipe,
  concatenation, and Kleene star.
  Those correspond to these set operations:
  for instance, if $R_0$ is a regular expression describing the language
  $\lang_0$, and $R_1$ describes $\lang_1$, then the regular expression
  $\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}$ describes $\lang_0\cup\lang_1$.
\end{proof}


%<*th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{theorem}  \label{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff}
  The collection of regular languages is closed under
  set complement, intersection, and set difference. 
\end{theorem}
%</th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

%<*pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>
\begin{proof}
  The  equation
  $S\cap \hat{S}=\setcomp{(\setcomp{S}\cup\setcomp{\smash{\hat{S}}\hspace{1.5pt}})}$
  describes set intersection in terms of union and complement.
  The prior lemma shows that regular languages are closed under union,
  so if we show they are closed under complement then this equation
  gives that they are also closed under intersection.
  Similarly, the equation
  $S-\hat{S}=S\cup\setcomp{\smash{\hat{S}}\hspace{1.5pt}}$ means that
  showing closure under complement will show also closure under 
  set difference.

  So to show that the complement of a regular language
  is also regular, fix a regular language~$\lang$.
  It is accepted by some Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
  % \Cref{sec:EquivDFSMAndNFSM} shows that deterministic and nondeterministic
  % Finite State machines recognize the same set of languages so without
  % loss of generality take~$\FSM$ to be deterministic.
  
  Define a new machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ with the same states and
  transition function as~$\FSM$ but whose accepting states are the
  complement,
  $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}-F_{\FSM}$.
  Because $\FSM$ is deterministic, each input string~$\tau$ is associated with a
  unique last state,~$q_{\tau}$, the state the machine is in after it
  consumes $\tau$'s last character.
  Moving from $\FSM$ to $\hat{\FSM}$ by taking the complement of the
  accepting states will complement the set of accepted strings, the language
  of the machine, because $q_{\tau}\in F_{\FSM}$ if and only if
  $q_{\tau}\notin F_{\hat{\FSM}}$.
  Thus the language of $\hat{\FSM}$ is the
  complement of the language of $\FSM$ and
  since this language is accepted by a Finite State machine, it too
  is regular.
\end{proof}
%</pf:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:RegLangEvenZerosMoreThanFourOnes}
  This language is regular.
  \begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s and more than two \str{1}'s}}
  \end{equation*}
  One way to show that is to produce a Finite State machine.
  Another is to spot that~$\lang$ is the intersection of 
  $\lang_0=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has an even number of \str{0}'s}}$
  and
  $\lang_1=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has more than two \str{1}'s}}$.
  Producing machines for those two is easy.
\end{example}
 
We now know that regular expressions, regular grammars,
deterministic finite state machines, and nondeterministic finite state
machines all describe the same set of languages, namely, the regular
languages.
\citetext{The fact that we can describe these languages in so many completely different
ways}{\autocite{SE:noperm}.} 
suggests that there's something natural and important about these
languages.
This is in analogy with the fact that the equivalence of Turing machines,
the lambda calculus, and all kinds of other things suggests that the
computable languages are natural and important.
They're not just an artifact of whatever random decisions the original
definer made.


\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Of course you must justify each answer.
\begin{exparts}
\item Every regular language is finite.
\item Over $\kleenestar{\B}$, the empty language is not regular.
\item Over $\kleenestar{\B}$, the language of all strings, $\kleenestar{\B}$,
  is not regular.
\item Every regular language is countable.
\item Every Finite State machine accepts at least one string.
\item For every Finite State machine there is one that has fewer
  states but accepts the same language.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item False.
The language $\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$ is regular, and is infinite.
\item False, the empty language is accepted by any Finite State machine
that has no accepting states.
\item False, the set of all strings is accepted by any Finite State machine
whose states are all accepting states.
\item False.
Over $\kleenestar{\B}$ the language of all strings $\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$
is regular, and is uncountable.
\item False, a Finite State machine with no accepting states does not
accept any strings.
\item False, a minimal machine cannot be further reduced.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
One of these is true and one is false.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts*}
\item Any finite language is regular.
\item Any regular language is finite.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
The first is true and the second is false.
\begin{exparts}
\item
If the language consists of the strings $\sigma_0$, \ldots $\sigma_{n-1}$
then a regular expression is
\re{$\sigma_0$|$\cdots$|$\sigma_{n-1}$}.
\item
The language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ described by the
regular expression \re{a*} is
$\set{\emptystring,\str{a},\str{aa},\ldots}$, and is infinite.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each language over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$ is regular.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ starts and ends with $\str{a}$}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{the number of $\str{a}$'s is even}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
One way is to give a regular expression for each language.
\begin{exparts}
\item \re{a(a|b)*a}
\item \re{b*(ab*a)*b*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false?
Justify your answer.
\begin{exparts}
\item If $\lang_0$ is a regular languages and $\lang_1\subseteq \lang_0$ then
  $\lang_1$ is also a regular languages.
\item  If $\lang_0$ is not regular and $\lang_0\subseteq \lang_1$ then
  $\lang_1$ is also not regular.
\item If $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$ is regular then each of the two is
  regular.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
Each is false.
\begin{exparts}
\item For $\lang_0$ use the set of all bitstrings, $\kleenestar{\B}$.
  For $\lang_1$ use any non-regular subset of it, such as
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\item For $lLang_0$ use any non-regular set of bitstrings, such as
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  For $\lang_1$ use $\kleenestar{\B}$.
\item For $\lang_0$ take any nonregular set of bitstrings, such as 
  $\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  For $\lang_1$ take its complement inside of $\kleenestar{\B}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
    Suppose that the language $\lang$ over $\B$ is regular.
    Show that the language
    $\hat{\lang}=\set{1\concat\sigma\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$,
    also over~$\B$, is also regular.
\end{exercise}

\recommended  
\begin{exercise}  % Rich p183
    Prove that the collection of regular languages over $\Sigma$
    is closed under each of the operations.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item $\operatorname{pref}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\sigma\concat\tau\in\lang$}}$ 
      \item $\operatorname{suff}(\lang)=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{there is a $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ such that $\tau\sigma\in\lang$}}$ 
    \end{exparts}    
  \end{exercise}

    \begin{exercise}
    The proof of \cref{th:RegLangsClosedCompIntDiff} works with
    deterministic Finite State machines.
    Find a nondeterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ such that
    producing another machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by
    taking the complement of the accepting states,
    $F_{\hat{\FSM}}=\setcomp{(F_{\FSM})}$,
    will not result in the langugage of the second machine
    being the complement of the language of the first.
    \begin{answer}
      For this machine the language of accepted strings contains~\str{1}.
      \begin{center}
        \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm57.pdf}
      \end{center}
      If we complement the set of accepting states then the language
      of the resulting machine also contains~\str{1}.
    \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Show that the set of regular languages is countable.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the language accepted by a Finite State machine with~$n$ states is 
infinite if and only if that machine accepts at least one string of length $k$,
where $n\leq k <2n$.  
\end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma\neq \emptyset$.
    Is the set of regular languages over~$\Sigma$ infinite?
    If so, is it countable?
    Is the set of languages over~$\Sigma$ that are not regular infinite?
    If so, is it countable?
  \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix two alphabets $\Sigma_0,\Sigma_1$.
A function $\map{h}{\Sigma_0}{\kleenestar{\Sigma_1}}$ induces a
\definend{homomorphism} on~$\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}$ via the operation
$h(\sigma\concat\tau)=h(\sigma)\concat h(\tau)$
(and $h(\emptystring)=\emptystring$).
\begin{exparts}
\item Take $\Sigma_0=\B$ and $\sigma_1=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$.
  Fix a homomorphism $\hat{h}(\str{0})=\str{a}$ and $\hat{h}(\str{1})=\str{ba}$.
  Find $\hat{h}(\str{01})$, 
  $\hat{h}(\str{10})$, 
  and $\hat{h}(\str{101})$.
\item Define $h(\lang)=\set{h(\sigma)\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma_0}}$.
  Let $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$;
  describe it with a regular expression.
  Using the homomorphism~$\hat{h}$ from the prior item, describe 
  $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$
  with a regular expression.
\item Prove that the collection of regular languages is closed under
  homomorphism, that if $\lang$ is regular then so is $h(\lang)$.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item $\hat{h}(\str{01})=\str{aba}$, 
  $\hat{h}(\str{10})=\str{baa}$,
  $\hat{h}(\str{101})=\str{baaba}$
\item A regular expression to describe 
  $\hat{\lang}=\set{\sigma\concat\str{1}\suchthat\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$
  is \re{(\str{0}|\str{1})*\str{1}}
  A regular expression to describe
  $\hat{h}(\hat{\lang})$ is \re{(a|ba)*ba}.
\item Let $\lang$ be described by the regular expression~$R$.
  Apply the function~$h$ to each non-meta symbol in~$R$.
  The result is a regular expression that describes $h(\lang)$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise}
    We will show that the class of regular languages is closed under
    reversal.
    Recall that the reversal of the language is
    defined to be the set of reversals of the strings in the language
    $\reversal{\lang}=\set{\reversal{\sigma}\suchthat \sigma\in\lang}$.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item Show that for any two strings the reversal of the concatenation
      is the concatenation, in the opposite order, of the reversals
      $\reversal{(\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1)}
         =\reversal{\sigma_1}\concat\reversal{\sigma_0}$.
    \textit{Hint:} you can use induction on the length of $\sigma_1$.
  \item One way to prove the result
    is to define a reversal operation on regular expressions.
    Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$.
    Let $\mathcal{R}$ be the set of regular expressions over that
    alphabet.
    Then we can define each of these:
    (i)~$\reversal{\emptyset}=\emptyset$
    (ii)~$\reversal{\emptystring}=\emptystring$
    (iii)~$\reversal(x)=x$ for any~$x\in\Sigma$
    (iv)~$\reversal{(R_0\concat R_1)}=\reversal{R_1}\concat\reversal{R_0}$
    (v)~$\reversal{\re{$R_0$|$R_1$}}=\re{$\reversal{R_0}$|$\reversal{R_1}$}$
    (vi)~$\reversal{\re{$R$*}}=\re{$\reversal{R}$*}$.
    (Note the relationship between (iv) and the prior exercise item.)
    Now show that this operation turns a regular expression for a language
    into a regular expression for the language's reversal.
  \item
    Another way to prove this is to reverse the machine.
    Take the language to be accepted by some machine~$\FSM$.
    Construct a new nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\hat{\FSM}$ by:
    (i)~giving $\hat{\FSM}$ all of $\FSM$'s states, so that
    $Q_{\hat{\FSM}}=Q_{\FSM}$.
    (ii)~adding to $\hat{\FSM}$ a new state~$s$, make $\varepsilon$
    transitions from it to all of $\FSM$'s accepting states,
    and make $s$ the start state of~$\hat{\FSM}$
    (iii)~reverse all of the transitions in the $\FSM$'s graph, 
    and
    (iv)~turn $\FSM$'s accepting states to non-accepting states, and
    turn $\FSM$'s start state to an accepting state.
    Show that $\hat{\FSM}$ accepts $\reversal{\lang}$.
  \end{exparts}
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
  Consider the set of powers of two $\set{2^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Show that there is a Finite State machine that accepts the
  collection of strings representing powers of~$2$ in binary.
  \item Show that there is no Finite State machine that accepts the
  collection of strings representing these numbers in decimal.
  \end{exparts}
  \begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item These strings have an inital~\str{1} and any subsequent characters are
  \str{0}'s.
  \item \url{https://cstheory.stackexchange.com/q/2083}
  \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{regular language|)}\index{language!regular|)}



% ================================================
\section{Languages that are not regular}\index{languages!non-regular|(}
Although Finite State machines are finite,
they can handle arbitrarily long inputs.
This chapter's first example, the power switch from
\Cref{ex:PowerSwitch}, has only two states
but even if we toggle it hundreds of times, it still keeps track of whether
the switch is on or off.
To handle these long inputs with only a small number of states, a machine
must revisit states, that is, it must loop.

%<*ex:FSMLoopingi>
Loops cause a pattern in what a machine accepts.
The diagram shows a machine that accepts~$\str{aabbbc}$
(it only shows some of the states, those that the machine traverses in
processing this input).
%</ex:FSMLoopingi>
\begin{equation*}  \label{discussion:FSMLooping}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm58.pdf}}
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
%<*ex:FSMLoopingii>
Besides $\str{aabbbc}$, this machine must also accept
$\str{a}(\str{abb})^2\str{bc}$ because that string 
takes the machine through the loop twice, and then to the accepting state. 
Likewise, this machine accepts $\str{a}(\str{abb})^3\str{bc}$,
and looping more times pumps out more accepted strings.
% (And, the string $\str{abc}$ bypassing the loop entirely 
% to end in the accepting state.)
%</ex:FSMLoopingii>

%<*th:PumpingLemma>
\begin{theorem}[Pumping lemma]\index{Pumping lemma}  \label{th:PumpingLemma}
Let~$\lang$ be a regular language.
Then there is a constant~$p\in\N$, the
\definend{pumping length}\index{pumping length} 
for the language,
such that every string~$\sigma\in \lang$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$
decomposes into three components
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ satisfying:~\begin{parenum}
\item the first two components are short, $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$,
\item $\beta$ is not empty, and 
\item all of the strings $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
$\alpha\beta^3\gamma$, \ldots{} are also members of the language~$\lang$.
\end{parenum}
\end{theorem}
%</th:PumpingLemma>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:PumpingLemmai>
  Suppose that $\lang$ is accepted by the
  Finite State machine~$\FSM$.
  Denote the number of states in $\FSM$ by~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\sequence{s_0,\ldots s_k}$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$.

  Finite State machines perform one transition per character 
  so the number of characters in an input string equals the number of 
  transitions.
  The number of (not necessarily distinct) states that the machine visits 
  is one more than the number of transitions. 
  Thus, in processing the first~$p$ characters of the input string~$\sigma$,
  the machine must visit some state more than once, that is, it must loop, 
  by the Pigeonhole Principle.
%</pf:PumpingLemmai>

%<*pf:PumpingLemmaii>
  Fix just such a repeated state~$q$.
  Fix the first two substrings of $\sigma$, $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i}$ and
  $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_j}$ with $i<j$ 
  that take the machine to state~$q$, meaning that $j$ is minimal such that
  the extended transition function gives
  $\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i})
     =\extended{\Delta}(\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_j})
     =q$.
  Let $\alpha$ be the string that first brings the machine to state~$q$, 
  namely $\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots, s_i}$
  Let $\beta$ be the string that brings the machine around the loop,
  $\sequence{s_{i+1},\,\ldots s_j}$. 
  Finally, let $\gamma$ be the rest, $\sequence{s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}$.
  (Possibly one or both of $\alpha$ and $\gamma$ is empty.)
  These strings satisfy conditions~(1) and~(2).
  (Choosing $q$ to be a state that is repeated 
  within the initial segment of~$\sigma$, and chosing $i$ and~$j$ to be minimal,
  guarantees that for instance if the 
  string~$\sigma$ brings machine around a loop a hundred times then we
  don't pick an $\alpha$ that includes the first ninety nine loops, and
  that therefore is longer than~$p$.)

  For condition~(3),
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\gamma=\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i,s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  brings the machine from the start state~$q_0$ to~$q$,
  and then to the same ending state as did $\sigma$.
  That is,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\gamma)=\extended{\Delta}(\alpha\beta\gamma)$
  and so is an accepting state.
  The other strings in~(3) are similar.
  For instance, for
  \begin{equation*}
    \alpha\concat\beta^2\concat\gamma
    =\alpha\beta\beta\gamma
      =\sequence{s_0,\,\ldots s_i,s_{i+1},\,\ldots, s_{j-1},s_{i+1},\,\ldots s_{j+1},\,\ldots s_k}
  \end{equation*}
  the substring $\alpha$ brings the machine from~$q_0$ to the state~$q$,
  the first $\beta$ brings it around to~$q$ again,
  then the second $\beta$ makes the machine loop to $q$ yet again,
  and finally $\gamma$ brings it to the same ending state as did $\sigma$.
%</pf:PumpingLemmaii>
\end{proof}

Typically we use the Pumping lemma to show that a language is
not regular, by assuming that it is regular and then using the lemma
to get a contradiction.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}
  The classic example is to 
  show that this language of matched parentheses is not regular.
  The alphabet is the set of the two parentheses
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{)},\str{(}}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \lang
    =\set{\str{(}^n\str{)}^n\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
        n\in\N}                                      
    =\set{\varepsilon, \str{()}, \str{(())}, \str{((()))}, 
       \str{(}^4\mspace{2mu}\str{)}^4,\, \ldots\,}       
  \end{equation*}
  For contradiction, assume that it is regular.
  Then the Pumping lemma says that $\lang$ has a pumping length,~$p$.

  Consider the string $\sigma=\str{(}^p\str{)}^p$.
  It is an element of~$\lang$ and 
  its length is greater than or equal to~$p$ so
  the Pumping lemma says that it
  decomposes into three substrings $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$
  satisfying the conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that
  the length of the prefix~$\alpha\concat\beta$ is less than or equal to~$p$.
  Thus both of the substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ are composed
  exclusively of open parentheses,~$\str{(}$'s.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string, so it
  contains at least one~$\str{(}$.

  Condition~(3) is that all of the strings
  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
  \ldots{}
  are members of~$\lang$.
  For the contradiction consider $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$.
  Compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, this string has an extra~$\beta$.
  That adds at least one open parenthesis, 
  without adding any balancing closed parentheses.
  In short, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more \str{(}'s than \str{)}'s and so
  it is not a member of the language~$\lang$. 
  But this contradicts the Pumping lemma, and 
  therefore the assumption that $\lang$ is regular is incorrect.
\end{example}

Matching parentheses, and other types of matching, is something that 
we often do.
For instance, compilers verify that parentheses match.
So that example shows that for many common computing tasks, regular languages
don't suffice.

\begin{example}
  Recall that a palindrome
  is a string that reads the
  same backwards as forwards, such as
  \str{bab}, \str{abbaabba},
  or~$\str{a}^5\str{b}\str{a}^5$.
  We will prove that the language
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
    \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
  of all palindromes over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ is
  not regular.

  For contradiction assume that this language is regular. 
  The Pumping lemma says that $\lang$ has a pumping length.
  Call it~$p$.
  
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p\in\lang$.
  That string has more than~$p$ characters so it decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$
  and so both substrings $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are composed entirely of
  \str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string and so
  $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  
  Condition~(3) is that the strings in the list
  $
    \alpha\gamma,   
    \alpha\beta^2\gamma,
    \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \ldots{}
  $ are also members of the language.
  We will get the contradiction by showing that the first one, $\alpha\gamma$,
  is not a palindrome.
  Compared to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, in $\alpha\gamma$ the $\beta$ is gone.
  The substring~$\beta$ does not have the~\str{b}, 
  because $\alpha$ and $\beta$ consist entirely of \str{a}'s, $\gamma$ has
  the~\str{b}.
  By the definition of $\sigma$, after that~\str{b} comes $\str{a}^p$.
  But, for $\alpha\gamma$ to be a palindrome, the~\str{b} must be preceeded by
  $p$-many \str{a}'s and by omitting~$\beta$ we've omitted at least one~\str{a}.
  So $\alpha\gamma$ is not a palindrome, which
  is a contradiction because the Pumping lemma says that the strings
  in the list are all members of~$\lang$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark} 
Don't make the mistake of thinking that the parts of $\sigma$ must match 
with the parts of its decomposition $\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$.
For instance, in the prior example a person might think that the 
Pumping lemma says that the decomposition
of $\str{a}^p\str{b}\str{a}^p$ into $\alpha\beta\gamma$
puts $\alpha$ as the initial $\str{a}^p$, etc.\sentencespace
But the Pumping lemma doesn't say that.
It only says that the first two strings together, $\alpha\concat\beta$, 
consists exclusively of \str{a}'s.
As far as the statement goes, 
the suffix $\gamma$ could well start with some $\str{a}$'s,
then followed by $\str{b}\str{a}^p$.
Likewise, in the matched parentheses example, the Pumping lemma does
not say that $\alpha\concat\beta$ gets all of the open parentheses, and
$\gamma$ could well have some.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSMCannotRecognizePlusOne}
  Consider 
  $
    \lang=\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m=n+1}
    =\set{\str{0}, \str{001}, \str{00011}, \ldots}
  $.
  Its members have one fewer \str{1} than~\str{0}.
  We will prove that it is not regular.

  For contradiction assume otherwise, that it is regular, and 
  set~$p$ as its pumping length.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{0}^{p+1}\str{1}^p\in\lang$.
  Because $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$, the Pumping lemma gives the decomposition
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ satisfying the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) says that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so 
  that the substrings $\alpha$ and~$\beta$ have only \str{0}'s.
  Condition~(2) says that $\beta$ has at least one character, necessarily
  a~\str{0}.
  Condition~(3) says that each of these is in the 
  language:~$\alpha\gamma$,
    $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$,
    $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$,
    \dots\sentencespace
  Consider the first, $\alpha\gamma$.
  By~(1) all of the \str{1}'s are in~$\gamma$.
  With that, 
  by~(2) the string $\alpha\gamma$ has fewer \str{0}'s then does $\sigma$
  but the same number of~\str{1}'s.
  That means $\alpha\gamma\notin\lang$, which
  contradicts the Pumping lemma.
\end{example}

We can interpret the prior example to say that Finite State machines
cannot correctly recognize a predecessor-successor relationship.
We can also use the Pumping lemma to show Finite State
machines cannot accept other arithmetic relations.

\begin{example}
The language
$  \lang  =\set{\str{a}^n\suchthat \text{$n$ is a perfect square} } 
         =\set{\emptystring,\, \str{a},\, \str{a}^4,\, \str{a}^9,\,\str{a}^{16},
                \, \ldots\,}$
is not regular.
For, suppose otherwise.  
Fix a pumping length~$p$ and consider $\sigma=\str{a}^{(p^2)}$,
so that $\lh{\sigma}=p^2$.

By the Pumping lemma, $\sigma$ decomposes into
$\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the
three conditions.
Condition~(1) 
is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, which implies that $\lh{\beta}\leq p$.
Condition~(2) is that $0<\lh{\beta}$.
Condition~(3) say that all of the strings  $\alpha\gamma$,
  $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \dots{}
are members of the language.

Consider the gap between the length
$\lh{\sigma}=\lh{\alpha\beta\gamma}$ and the length
$\lh{\alpha\beta^2\gamma}$.
Because $0<\lh{\beta}\leq p$, that gap is at most~$p$.
But the definition of the language is that after $\sigma$ the next-longest
string has length $(p+1)^2=p^2+2p+1$, which is strictly greater than~$p$.
Thus the length of $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not a perfect square, which 
contradicts the Pumping lemma's assertion that $\alpha\beta^2\gamma\in\lang$.
\end{example}

Sometimes we can use the Pumping lemma in conjunction with
the closure properties of regular languages to solve problems.

\begin{example}
  The language
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
    \text{$\sigma$ has as many \str{a}'s as \str{b}'s}}$
  is not regular.
  To prove that, note that the language
  $\hat{\lang}=\set{\str{a}^m\str{b}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat m,n\in\N}$ is clearly
  regular, described by the regular expression~\re{a*b*}, 
  and recall that the intersection of two regular languages is regular.
  But $\lang\cap\hat{\lang}$ is the set
  $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
  and \cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} shows it isn't regular,
  after we substitute \str{a} and~\str{b} for the parentheses.
\end{example}

In previous sections we saw how to show that a language is regular,
either by producing a Finite State machine that accepts it or
by producing a regular expression that describes it.
Being able to show that a language is not regular nicely balances that. 
But our interest is motivated by more than symmetry.
A Turing machine can solve the problem 
of \cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang}, of accepting
strings of balanced parentheses, but we now know that a
Finite State machine cannot.
Therefore we now know that to solve this problem we need scratch memory.
So the results in this section speak to the resources needed to solve 
the problems.


\begin{exercises}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:BalancedParenFirstStringNoGood}
\Cref{ex:BalancedParensIsNotRegularLang} uses $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$
to show that the language of
balanced parentheses is not regular.
Instead get the contradiction by showing that the first string on the list,
$\alpha\gamma$, is not a member of the language.
\begin{answer}
The omission of $\beta$ means that the number of 
open parentheses,~\str{(}, is at least one fewer than the number of 
closed parentheses,~\str{)}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
    For each, give five strings that are elements of the language 
    and five that are not, and then show that the language is not regular.
    \begin{exparts*}
    \item $\lang_0=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n+2=m}$
    \item $\lang_1=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\str{c}^n\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
    \item $\lang_2=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\suchthat n<m}$
    \end{exparts*}    
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Five elements of $\lang_0$ are \str{bb}, \str{abbb}, \str{aabbbb}, 
$\str{a}^3\str{b}^5$, and~$\str{a}^4\str{b}^6$.
Five strings that are not elements are
\emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{aabbb}, and~\str{baabbbb}. 

Assume for contradiction that $\lang_0$ is regular.
By the Pumping lemma it has a pumping length,~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+2}$.
The Pumping lemma gives a decomposition 
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so these two substrings 
contain only~\str{a}'s.
Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is nonempty, so it consists of at least 
one~\str{a}.
Condition~(3) is that the strings 
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all members of
the language~$\lang_0$.
But now $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more~\str{a}'s, but no more~\str{b}'s,
than does $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$.
That means it is not in the language~$\lang_0$, a contradiction.

\item In prose, the description of~$\lang_1$ is that it consists of 
successive \str{a}'s, \str{b}'s, and~\str{c}'s,
with the only the restriction that the number of 
\str{a}'s equals the number of~\str{c}'s.
Thus five elements of $\lang_1$ are:~\emptystring, 
\str{ac}, \str{b}, \str{aabcc}, and~\str{bb}. 
Five that are not are:~\str{aac}, \str{acc}, \str{ab}, \str{bbc}, and~\str{c}.

Assume for contradiction that it is regular.
Then it has a pumping length~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{c}^p$
Because $\sigma\in\lang_1$ and $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$, the Pumping lemma
says that it decomposes $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ 
in a way that satisfies the three conditions.
By condition~(1) the first two substrings together are short, 
$\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$.
Thus these two substrings consist entirely of~$\str{a}$'s.
By condition~(2) the second substring~$\beta$ is nonempty,
so it consists of at least one~\str{a}.
Finally, condition~(3) gives that the strings 
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all members of
the language~$\lang_1$.
But the string~$\alpha\gamma$, when compared with $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
has at least one fewer~\str{a} but the same number of~\str{c}'s.
That means it is not a member of the language, which is a contradiction.

\item Five elements of $\lang_2$ are \str{abb}, \str{abbb}, \str{abbbb}, 
$\str{a}\str{b}^5$, and~$\str{a}^4\str{b}^5$.
Five strings that are not elements are
\emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{aab}, and~\str{baabbb}. 

Suppose that $\lang_2$ is regular and fix a pumping length,~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+1}$.
The Pumping lemma gives a decomposition 
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, so these two substrings 
contain only~\str{a}'s.
Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is nonempty, so it consists of at least 
one~\str{a}.
Condition~(3) is that the strings 
$\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are members of~$\lang_2$.
Observe that, compared with $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^{p+1}$,
$\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ has more~\str{a}'s, but no more~\str{b}'s.
Thus the number of \str{a}'s is at least~$p+1$ and the number of \str{b}'s
is fixed at~$p+1$.
That means that $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$ is not in the language~$\lang_2$, 
which is a contradiction.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the language over $\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$
consisting of strings having more \str{a}'s than \str{b}'s is not
regular.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each language, produce five strings that are members.
Then decide if that language is regular.
You must prove each assertion by either producing a
regular expression or using the Pumping lemma.
Assume that $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\set{\alpha\concat\alpha\suchthat \alpha\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}}$.
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n=m}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n,m\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat n+3=m}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat m-n>12}$
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular and one is not.
Which is which?
(You must prove your assertions.)
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat n=m^2}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat 3<m,n}$
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Use the Pumping lemma to prove that
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^{m-1}\str{c}\str{b}^m\suchthat m\in\N^+}$
is not regular.
(It may help to first produce five strings from the language.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is this 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B} \suchthat 
  \text{$\sigma=\alpha\beta\reversal{\alpha}$ for $\alpha,\beta\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$ regular?
Either way, prove it.
\begin{answer} % David Richerby https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/97885/67754
The language is regular.
It is a trick question:~take $\alpha=\emptystring$ to get that the set equals
$\kleenestar{\B}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{1}}}
            \suchthat \text{$\lh{\sigma}=n!$ for some $n\in\N$}}$
is not regular.
\hint~the differences $(n+1)!-n!$ grow without bound.
\end{exercise}  
  
\begin{exercise}
One of these is regular, one is not:
$\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat m,n\in\N}$ and
$\set{\str{0}^n\str{1}\str{0}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
Which is which?
Of course, you must prove your assertions.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a Finite State machine that accepts this language
of all sums totalling less than four,
$\lang_4=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$ and $k<4$.}}$.
Use the Pumping lemma to show that no Finite State machine accepts the language
of all sums,
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$.}}$.
\begin{answer}
The language $\lang_4=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$ and $k<4$.}}$ is finite, and any finite language is regular.

As to the language if all sums, 
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^k\suchthat \text{$i,j,k\in\N$ and $i+j=k$.}}$, 
suppose that it is regular and fix a pumping length~$p$.
Consider $\sigma=a^pb^0c^p$.
Because $|\sigma|\geq p$ it has a decomposition 
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$ subject to the three conditions
of the Pumping lemma.
By the first condition the length $|\alpha\beta|$ is less than or equal to~$p$,
and so consists entirely of \str{a}'s.
By the second condition the string~$\beta$ is nonempty and so consists of at 
least one~\str{a}.
The third condition says that $\alpha\gamma$ is also a member of the language,
but that is a contradiction since this string has fewer \str{a}'s than 
\str{c}'s, and no \str{b}'s, and thus is not a member of the language.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Rebut someone who says to you, 
``Sure, for the machine in the introduction
on \cpageref{discussion:FSMLooping},
a single loop will cause $\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma$.
But if you had a double
loop like below then you need a longer decomposition.''
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa51.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
The proof of the Pumping lemma uses the Pigeonhole Principle to show that 
there is at least one loop and 
it then reasons with that loop.
It doesn't rely on there being no later loop.

More explicitly, the Pumping lemma says that we can take the pumping length
to be the number of states in any machine that accepts the language.
So set $p=9$.
If $\sigma=\str{aabbabbaba}$ then because $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$ and 
$\sigma\in\lang$, we know there is a decomposition satisfing the three
conditions.  
One such is $\alpha=\str{a}$, $\beta=\str{abbabb}$, and~$\gamma=\str{aba}$.
Checking that (1)~$\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$, that (2)~$\beta\neq\emptystring$,
and (3)~the strings $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, \ldots{} are all
members of~$\lang$.

If $\sigma=\str{a}(\str{abb})^{200}\str{aba}$ then again 
because $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$ and $\sigma\in\lang$, 
we know there is a decomposition satisfing the three conditions.  
One such is $\alpha=\str{a}$, $\beta=\str{abb}$, 
and~$\gamma=(\str{abb})^{199}\str{aba}$.
Checking the three conditions is routine.

Finally, if $\sigma=\str{aabbbabbaa}$ then
we can take $\alpha=\str{aab}$, $\beta=\str{bbabba}$, and~$\gamma=\str{a}$.
Or, we could instead take 
$\alpha=\str{aab}$, $\beta=\str{bba}$, and~$\gamma=\str{bbaa}$.
Again, checking the three conditions is routine.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % Rich, p 182
Decide if each is a regular language of bitstrings:
\begin{exparts*}
\item the number of \str{0}'s plus the number of \str{1}'s equals five,
\item the number of \str{0}'s minus the number of \str{1}'s equals five
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that
$\set{\str{0}^m\str{1}^n\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat m\neq n}$
is not regular.
\textit{Hint:} use the closure properties of regular languages.
\end{exercise}

    
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:NoFSMAcceptsPrimes}
Show that 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
\text{$\sigma=\str{1}^n$ where $n$ is prime}}$
is not a regular language.
\hint~in a sequence of numbers with a constant positive difference,
some term is not prime. 
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider 
$\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\str{c}^{i\cdot j}\suchthat i,j\in\N}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Give five strings from this language.
\item Use the Pumping lemma to show that it is not regular.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The number of \str{c}'s is the product of the number of
  \str{a}'s and the number of \str{b}'s.
  Five such strings are \str{abc}, \str{abbcc}, \str{abbbccc},
  \str{aabcc}, and~\str{aabbcccc}.
\item
  For contradiction assume that this language is regular. 
  The Pumping lemma says that $\lang$ has a pumping length,~$p$.
  Consider $\sigma=\str{a}^p\str{b}^p\str{c}^{(p^2)}$.
  That string has more than~$p$ characters so it decomposes as
  $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, subject to the three conditions.
  Condition~(1) is that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq p$
  and so both substrings $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are composed entirely of
  \str{a}'s.
  Condition~(2) is that $\beta$ is not the empty string and so
  $\beta$ consists of at least one~\str{a}.
  
  Condition~(3) is that the strings in the list
  $
    \alpha\gamma,   
    \alpha\beta^2\gamma,
    \alpha\beta^3\gamma,
    \ldots{}
  $ are also members of the language.
  We will get the contradiction from the first one, $\alpha\gamma$.
  Compared to $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$, in the first one the $\beta$ is gone.
  So the number of \str{a}'s in $\alpha\gamma$ is less than the number 
  in $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$,
  But the number of \str{b}'s and the number of \str{c}'s is the same. 
  Thus in $\alpha\gamma$ the number of \str{a}'s times the number of
  \str{b}'s does not equal the number of \str{c}'s.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The language~$\lang$ described by the regular expression \re{a*bbbb*}
is a regular language.
We can apply the Pumping lemma to it.
The proof of the Pumping lemma says that for the pumping length we can use
the number of states in a machine that accepts the language.
Here that gives $p=4$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider $\sigma=\str{abbb}$.
Give a decomposition $\sigma=\alpha\beta\gamma$ that satisfies the 
three conditions.
\item Do the same for $\sigma=\str{b}^{15}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
We need that $\lh{\alpha\beta}\leq 4$, that $\lh{\beta}>0$, and that the 
strings $\alpha\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^2\gamma$, $\alpha\beta^3\gamma$, \ldots{}
are members of~$\lang$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\alpha=\emptystring$, $\beta=\str{a}$, and~$\gamma=\str{bbb}$
\item $\alpha=\emptystring$, $\beta=\str{b}$, and~$\gamma=\str{b}^{14}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % https://math.stackexchange.com/a/1508488 (Brian M Scott)
For a regular language, a pumping length~$p$ is a number 
with the property that every word of length~$p$ or more can be pumped,
that is, can be decomposed so that it satisfies the three properties of 
\cref{th:PumpingLemma}. 
The proof of that theorem shows that where a Finite State
machine recognizes the language, the number of states in the machine suffices
as a pumping length.
But~$p$ can be smaller.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider the language $\lang$ described by \re{(\str{0}\str{1})*}.
  Construct a deterministic 
  Finite State machine with three states that accepts this language.
\item Show that the minimal pumping length for~$\lang$ is $1$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This works.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa50.pdf}
\end{center}
\item The prior item shows that 
there is a Finite State machine accepting this language that has three states.
So, any non-empty word of the language must cause it to repeat a state, 
and therefore can be pumped. 
The minimum length of a non-empty word of the language is~$2$ 
so the minimum pumping length is at most $2$.
However the language contains no word of length~$1$ so if $\sigma\in\lang$ 
and $|\sigma|\geq 1$ then automatically $|\sigma\geq 2|$,
and so $\sigma$ can be pumped. 
Therefore the minimum pumping length for the language is~$1$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

  % \begin{exercise}

Nondeterministic Finite State machines can always be made to have a single
