% ...................................................
\section{Minimization}\index{minimization of a Finite State machine|(}\index{Finite State machine!minimization|(}
Contrast these two Finite State machines.
For each, the language of accepted strings is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{0} and at least one \str{1}}}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa16.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}}
  \tag{*}
\end{equation*}
Our experience from making machines is that the states should have a meaning.
For instance, on the left $q_2$ means something like,
``have seen the needed at least one \str{1} but still
waiting for a~\str{0}.''
In a properly designed machine, input puts the machine in state~$q_2$
if indeed that input has so far had at least one~\str{1} but no~\str{0}. 

The machine on the right doesn't satisfy this design principle because
the meaning of $q_4$ is the same as that of~$q_2$, 
and $q_3$'s meaning is the same as~$q_5$'s.
That is, the pairs of states have the same future.
This machine has redundant states.

We will give an algorithm
that starts with a Finite State machine and
from it finds the smallest machine that accepts the same language.
The algorithm works by
by collapsing together redundant states.

\begin{definition}  \label{def:distinguishable}
In a Finite State machine over~$\Sigma$,
where $n\in\N$ we say that two states $q,\hat{q}$ are
\definend{$n$-distinguishable}\index{n distinguishable states@$n$-distinguishable states}\index{states!n distinguishable@$n$-distinguishable}
if there is a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ with
$\lh{\sigma}\leq n$ such that starting the machine
in state~$q$ and giving it input~$\sigma$ ends in an accepting state
while starting it in~$\hat{q}$ and giving it~$\sigma$ does not,
or vice versa.
Otherwise the states are 
\definend{$n$-indistinguishable, $q\sim_{n}\hat{q}$}.\index{n indistinguishable states@$n$-indistinguishable states}\index{states!n indistinguishable@$n$-indistinguishable}

Two states $q,\hat{q}$ are \definend{distinguishable}\index{distinguishable states}\index{states!distinguishable}
if there is an~$n$ for which they are $n$-distinguishable.
Otherwise they are \definend{indistinguishable, $q\sim\hat{q}$}.\index{distinguishable states}\index{states!distinguishable}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
  Starting the machine on the left above in state~$q_0$
  and feeding it~$\sigma=\str{0}$ ends
  in the non-accepting state~$q_1$,
  while starting it in~$q_2$ and processing the same input ends
  in the accepting state~$q_3$.
  So $q_0$ and~$q_2$ are $n=1$-distinguishable, and therefore are
  distinguishable.
  
  Also, $q_2$ and $q_3$ are $0$-distinguishable, via~$\sigma=\emptystring$.
  Any state that is not accepting is $0$-distinguishable from any
  state that is accepting.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
  For the machine on the right,
  the states $q_2$ and~$q_4$ are indistinguishable.
  The set of input strings that bring the
  machine from~$q_2$ to an accepting state is
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has the form \re{0*|(1*0*)}}}$.
  % (Below we will see an algorithm but here we just work by inspection.)
  For state~$q_4$ the set of strings taking
  it to an accepting state satisfies the same regular expression.

  The states that the machine is brought to
  need not be equal, it is a matter of whether they are accepting or not.
  For instance, if $\sigma=\str{0}$
  then starting at $q_2$ takes the
  machine to $q_3$ while starting at $q_4$ takes the machine to $q_5$.
  This is not a distinguishing string because while the states aren't
  equal, both are accepting.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}
  The $\sim$ relation and the $\sim_{n}$ relations
  are equivalences.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \cref{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}.  
\end{proof}

Our algorithm\footnote{%
  This is
  \protect\definend{Moore's algorithm}.  
  It is easy to understand and suitable for
  small calculations but if you are writing code then 
  \protect\citetext{be aware that another algorithm}{%
    See \protect\autocite{Knuutila}.}, \protect\definend{Hopcroft's algorithm}, 
  is better, albeit more complex.}
first finds all states that are
distinguishable by the length~zero string,
next finds all states distinguishable by length zero or one
strings, etc.
At the end the machine's states are broken into classes where
inside each class the states are indistinguishable by strings of any length.
Those classes serve as the states of the minimal machine.
We first outline the steps,
then we will work through two complete examples

% \begin{center} \label{page:MachineWithRedundantStates}
%   \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}
% \end{center}
So consider again
the machine with redundant states that we saw in ($*$)~above.
The states that are indistinguishable by the length zero
string~$\emptystring$
fall into two classes,
the non-accepting states $Q-F=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}$
accepting states $F=\set{q_3,q_5}$.
We use this notation for the equivalence classes
of the $\sim_{0}$~relation.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{0,0}=Q-F=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{0,1}=F=\set{q_3,q_5}
\end{equation*}

Next we find the classes of states that are $1$-indinstinguishable, that
are indistinguishable by either length zero or length one strings.
One way to proceed is:~for each state we find where putting the 
machine in that state
and feeding it the length zero string~$\emptystring$
and the length one strings \str{0} and~\str{1} will take
the machine.
In `take the machine' 
we only care whether the resulting state is 
a member of $\eclass_{0,0}$ or of~$\eclass_{0,1}$. 
(The first column repeats the prior paragraph. 
We'll streamline the algorithm below.)
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
           &$\emptystring$  &\str{0}           &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-4}
    $q_0$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$   &$\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_1$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$   &$\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_2$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$   &$\eclass_{0,0}$    \\
    $q_3$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$   &$\eclass_{0,1}$    \\
    $q_4$  &$\eclass_{0,0}$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$   &$\eclass_{0,0}$     \\
    $q_5$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$  &$\eclass_{0,1}$   &$\eclass_{0,1}$    \\      
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
Some states are distinguishable, some are not.
For instance, $q_0$ and $q_1$ are $1$-distinguishable
by the input string~\str{1}, while
and $q_1$ and $q_2$ are $1$-distinguishable by~\str{0}.
But $q_2$ and~$q_4$ are $1$-indistinguishable.
These are the $\sim_{1}$ equivalence classes.
\begin{equation*}
  \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
  \tag{$**$}
\end{equation*}
The states that we spotted by eye as redundant, $q_2,q_4$ and~$q_3,q_5$ 
continue to be together.

To get the $\sim_{2}$ classes
we may think to expand the table to add four columns, one for each
of \str{00}, \str{01}, \str{10}, and~\str{11}.
But proceeding in this way, at the next step doing all strings of length
three strings, etc., would make the time to do the job grow exponentially
since there are $2^k$ strings of length~$k$.

The way to streamline the algorithm
is to ask how two states $q,\hat{q}$ could be
$2$-distinguishable but not $1$-distinguishable.
This happens only if, where the distinguishing string is $\sigma$, 
the two states are distinguished by $\sigma$'s final character,
simply because if an earlier character would do then they would be
$1$-distinguishable.

In general, let the states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ be
$n+1$-distinguishable but not $n$-distinguishable. 
Suppose that a distinguishing string is 
$
 \sigma=\sequence{s_0,s_1,\,\ldots s_{n-1},s_n}=\tau\concat s_n 
$.
Because the states are not $n$-distinguishable,
where the prefix $\tau$ brings the machine from $q$ to a state~$r$ in some
class~$\eclass_{n,i}$, then
$\tau$ must bring the machine from $\hat{q}$ to some $\hat{r}$
in the same class,~$\eclass_{n,i}$. 
So the distinguishing must involve $\sigma$'s final character,~$s_n$,
taking~$r$ to a state in one class and taking~$\hat{r}$ to 
a state in another.

Therefore, at each step we don't need to test whole strings, 
we need only test single characters to see if 
they split the equivalence classes, the $\eclass_{n,i}$'s.
And we need only test classes with more than one character, because
singleton classes cannot be split.
These are the relevant single-character computations.
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
    &\str{0}  &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_2$      &$\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,2}$    \\
    $q_4$      &$\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,2}$    
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \qquad  
  \begin{transitionfunction}[]
    &\str{0}  &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_3$      &$\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,3}$    \\
    $q_5$      &$\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,3}$    
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
In neither case is there a split
so the algorithm stops. 
The examples below add a table notation that simplifies the
computation.

\begin{example}
  We will find a machine that accepts the same language as this one but
  that has a minimimum number of states.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}
  \end{center}
  To do bookkeeping we will use triangular
  tables like the one below.
  They have an entry for 
  every two-element set of state indices $\set{i,j}$
  with $i,j\in\set{0,\,\ldots 5}$ and  $i\neq j$.
  
  Start by marking the $i,j$~entries for
  state pairs that are $0$-distinguishable, that is, 
  where one of $q_i,q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{center}
   The blanks entries
   mark state pairs that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   So~$\sim_{0}$, which as a binary relation is a set of pairs,
   is below.
   (The table doesn't bother with the $q_i,q_i$ boxes, because
   a state is indistinguishable from itself.
   In the relation below these are written $\set{q_0,q_0}$. 
   Of course in a set repeats collapse so we could rewrite, e.g.,
   $\set{q_0,q_0}$ as $\set{q_0}$,
   but as written they are correct and also fit with
   \cref{lem:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm}.)
   \begin{multline*}
     \sim_{0}=\bigl\{\set{q_0,q_0},\,\set{q_0,q_3},\,\set{q_0,q_4},\,
       \set{q_3,q_3},\,\set{q_3,q_4},\,\set{q_4,q_4}, \bigr.       \\
     \bigl. \set{q_1,q_1},\,\set{q_1,q_2},\,\set{q_1,q_5},\,
     \set{q_2,q_2},\,\set{q_2,q_5},\,\set{q_5,q_5} \bigr\}
   \end{multline*}
   Form the $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes by 
   finding entries~$i,j$ that are blank, and then $q_i$ is $0$-equivalent
   to~$q_j$.
   For instance, the equivalence class containing $q_0$ also contains
   $q_3$ and~$q_4$ because the boxes $0,2$ and~$0,3$ are blank.
   Similarly, the equivalence class containing $q_2$ also contains $q_1$
   and~$q_5$.
   Here are the two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{0,3,4}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{1,2,5}        
   \end{equation*}

  Iterate.
  Look at the table's blanks, the $0$-indistinguishable pairs of states,
  to see if they can be $1$-distinguished.
  On the left below is each $0$-indistinguishable pair along with the
  pair to which the machine takes it with the listed input character.
  For instance, the input \str{a} takes the machine's state $q_0$ 
  to~$q_1$, and takes $q_3$ to~$q_5$.
  If the resulting pair is $0$-distinguishable then mark it.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $\set{0,3}$  &$\set{1,5}$  &$\set{2,5}$  \\
      $\set{0,4}$  &$\set{1,5}$  &$\set{2,5}$  \\
      $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{3,4}$  &$\set{4,3}$  \\
      $\set{1,5}$  &$\set{3,5}$ \ck  &$\set{4,5}$ \ck  \\
      $\set{2,5}$  &$\set{4,5}$ \ck  &$\set{3,5}$ \ck  \\
      $\set{3,4}$  &$\set{5,5}$   &$\set{5,5}$  \\                              
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    % \begin{varwidth}{0.19\linewidth}
    %   \begin{rulealigned*}
    %     \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0,3,4} \\
    %     \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
    %     \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{5}   
    %   \end{rulealigned*}
    % \end{varwidth}
    \equivclasses{%
        \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0,3,4} \\
        \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
        \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{5}   
    }
  \end{center}
  So this round finds that $q_1$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_5$
  and that $q_2$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_5$.
  The triangular table shows the additional marks, and the
  $1$-equivalence classes also reflect that.

  The next iteration subdivides the $\sim_{1}$-equivalence classes,
  the $\eclass_{1,i}$'s, to compute the $\sim_{2}$-equivalence classes.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $\set{0,3}$  &$\set{1,5}$ \ck  &$\set{2,5}$ \ck \\
      $\set{0,4}$  &$\set{1,5}$ \ck &$\set{2,5}$ \ck \\
      $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{3,4}$  &$\set{4,3}$  \\
      $\set{3,4}$  &$\set{5,5}$   &$\set{5,5}$  \\                              
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    % \begin{varwidth}{0.2\linewidth}
    %   \dropeqnvertspace
    %   \begin{align*}
    %     \eclass_{2,0} &=\set{0} \\
    %     \eclass_{2,1} &=\set{3,4} \\
    %     \eclass_{2,2} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
    %     \eclass_{2,3} &=\set{5}   
    %   \end{align*}
    % \end{varwidth}
    \equivclasses{\eclass_{2,0} &=\set{0} \\
        \eclass_{2,1} &=\set{3,4} \\
        \eclass_{2,2} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
        \eclass_{2,3} &=\set{5}   
      }
  \end{center}

  Running through one more iteration gives  
  no more splitting so the process stops.
  Here is the minimized machine with $s_0$ for $\eclass_{2,0}$, etc.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min04.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This machine's start state is the one containing the original machine's
  start, $q_0$.
  This machine's final states are the ones containing the final states of the
  original machine.

  As to the arrows, for instance consider this machine's arrow
  labelled~\str{a} from $s_1$ to~$s_3$.
  On this machine $s_1$ represents the class~$\eclass_1=\set{q_3,q_4}$.
  Look on the starting machine at the arrows leaving those states and
  labelled~\str{a}.
  Both arrows go to $q_5$, which is contained in
  the equivalence class~$s_3=\eclass_3$.
\end{example}

The algorithm has one more step, which was not needed in the prior example.
If the machine has any states that are unreachable from~$q_0$ then we
omit those.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SecondMinimization}
  Minimize this machine.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min00.pdf}
  \end{center}
  First,~$q_5$ cannot be reached from the start state.
  Drop it.
  That leaves this initial triangular table.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    \equivclasses{%
        \eclass_{0,0} &=\set{0,1,2} \\
        \eclass_{0,1} &=\set{3,4}
    }
  \end{center}
  Split the $0$-equivalence classes to make the $1$-equivalence classes.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $\set{0,1}$  &$\set{1,2}$  &$\set{2,3}$ \ck \\
      $\set{0,2}$  &$\set{1,2}$  &$\set{2,4}$ \ck \\
      $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{2,2}$  &$\set{3,4}$  \\
      $\set{3,4}$  &$\set{3,4}$  &$\set{3,4}$  \\                              
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    \equivclasses{%
        \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0} \\
        \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{1,2}   \\ 
        \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{3,4}   
    }
  \end{center}
  No additional splitting happens on the next iteration so the process stops.
  The minimized machine has three states.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min05.pdf}
  \end{center}
\end{example}

This result verifies that the algorithm computes what we want,
the $\sim$ equivalence classes.

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm}
  Let $\FSM$ be a Finite State machine over the alphabet~$\Sigma$.
  For this sequence of binary relations among the states
  \begin{align*}
    \mathcal{R}_0&=\set{\set{q,\hat{q}}\in Q_{\FSM}^2\suchthat \text{both are accepting or neither is accepting}}   \\
                 &\shortvdotswithin{=}   
    \mathcal{R}_{k+1}&=\set{\set{q,\hat{q}}\in\mathcal{R}_{k}\suchthat
      \text{$\set{\Delta(q,x),\Delta(\hat{q},x)}\in\mathcal{R}_{k}$
      for all $x\in\Sigma$}}
  \end{align*}
  these properties hold:
  \begin{parenum}
  \item the binary relation $\mathcal{R}_k$ equals the binary
    relation~$\sim_{k}$
  \item $\mathcal{R}_0\supseteq \mathcal{R}_1\supseteq \cdots$
  \item there is an~$n\in\set{0,\,\ldots\, |Q_{\FSM}|}$ such
    that $\mathcal{R}_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}_n$, and furthermore,
    $\mathcal{R}_k=\mathcal{R}_n$ for all $k\geq n$
  \item if $\mathcal{R}_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}_{n}$ then $\mathcal{R}_{n}$
    is the $\sim$ relation.
  \end{parenum}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Half of property~(1) is to  
  show that if two states are
  $\mathcal{R}_k$-distinguishable then they are $\sim_{k}$-distinguishable.
  We do induction on~$k$.
  For the $k=0$ base step, \cref{def:distinguishable}
  says that states are
  $0$~indistinguishable if and only if either both are accepting states
  or neither is, which is the definition of~$\mathcal{R}_{0}$.
  The inductive step supposes that $\mathcal{R}_{i}$ equals $\sim_{i}$
  for all $i\leq k$  
  and considers the $k+1$~case.
  Let  $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ be $\mathcal{R}_{k+1}$~distinguishable.
  Then there is a character $x\in\Sigma$ such that the pair
  $\set{\Delta(q,x), \Delta(\hat{q},x)}$ was marked at some
  previous iteration, $i$.
  The inductive hypothesis says there is a string $\tau$ of length at most~$k$
  that $\sim_{k}$~distinguishes $\Delta(q,x)$ from~$\Delta(\hat{q},x)$.
  Then the string $\sigma=x\concat\tau$ has length at most~$k+1$ and
  will $\sim_{k+1}$~distinguish $q$ from $\hat{q}$.
  
  The other half of~(1) is to show
  that if two states are are $\sim_{k+1}$-distinguishable. 
  then they are $R_{k+1}$-distinguishable.
  We will use induction
  on the length~$k$ of the distinguishing string.
  The $k=0$ base step is again
  that $\sim_{0}$ and~$\mathcal{R}_0$ are equal by definition.
  For the inductive step suppose that the length of $\sigma$ is $\ell+1$
  and suppose also the hypothesis
  that for all $j\leq \ell$ if two states are
  $\sim_{j}$-distintguishable then they are $\mathcal{R}_{j}$-distinguishable.
  Let $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ be $\sim_{k+1}$-distinguishable,
  via the string~$\sigma$.
  Decompose $\sigma$ as $\sigma=x\concat\sigma_{0}$, for $x\in\Sigma$.
  Apply the inductive hypothesis,
  giving that the pair $\set{\Delta(q,x),\Delta(\hat{q},x)}$
  is $\mathcal{R}_{k}$-distinguishable,
  and so they are in different $\mathcal{R}_{k}$ classes.
  That implies that
  the pair $q,\hat{q}$ is $\mathcal{R}_{k+1}$-distinguishable,
  via the character~$x$.

  Property~(2) holds because all of the elements of
  $\mathcal{R}_{k+1}$ are elements of~$\mathcal{R}_k$, by definition.
  
  For the first half of~(3),
  as the examples illustrate, each iteration splits 
  equivalence classes and that can only happen
  each states is alone in its class.
  The worst case
  starts with a single class and at each iteration pulls out
  one state.
  The ``furthermore'' of~(3) holds because if none of the
  two-element sets of $\mathcal{R}_{n+1}$ differ from those of~$\mathcal{R}_n$
  then they must be the same at the next iteration,
  simply because it is the same operation.

  Property~(4) follows from the prior properties, and
  \cref{def:distinguishable}.
  See \cref{exer:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm}.
  \end{proof}
  

\begin{exercises}
  \begin{exercise}
    What happens when you minimize a machine that is already minimal?
    \begin{answer}
      Not a hell of a lot.
      You get back the original machine, in the sense that
      you get $s_0=\mathcal{E}_{n,0}=\set{0}$,
      $s_1=\mathcal{E}_{n,1}=\set{1}$, etc. 
    \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}


  \recommended
  \begin{exercise}
    Perform the minimization algorithm using the triangluar tables
    on the machine with redundant states
    at the start of this section.
  \end{exercise}
  
  \recommended
  \begin{exercise}
  Minimize these two.
  \begin{exparts}
    \item\quad \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min01.pdf}}
    \item\quad \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min02.pdf}}
  \end{exparts}
  \end{exercise}

  \recommended
  \begin{exercise}
    What happens if you perform the minimization procedure in
    \cref{ex:SecondMinimization} without first omitting the
    unreachable state?
  \end{exercise}

  \begin{exercise}
    \cref{def:distinguishable} defines a binary relation~$\sim$ between states.
    Show that it is an equivalence relation.
  \end{exercise}

  \recommended
  \begin{exercise}
    Minimize.
    \begin{center}
      \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min06.pdf}
    \end{center}
    Note that the algorithm takes, roughly, a number of steps that
    are equal to the number of states in the machine.
    \begin{answer}
      This is the initial triangular table.
      \begin{center} 
        \begin{minimizetable}{5}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
          \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
          \cline{2-2}
          &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
          \cline{3-3}
          &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
          \cline{4-4}
          \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
          \cline{1-4}      
        \end{minimizetable}
        \qquad
        \equivclasses{%
          \eclass_{0,0} &=\set{0,1,2,3} \\
          \eclass_{0,1} &=\set{4}
        }
      \end{center}
      Divide the $0$-equivalence classes into $1$-equivalence classes.
      \begin{center} \small
        \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
          \cline{2-3}
          $\set{0,1}$  &$\set{1,2}$  &$\set{0,1}$  \\
          $\set{0,2}$  &$\set{1,3}$  &$\set{0,2}$  \\
          $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{2,3}$  &$\set{1,2}$  \\
          $\set{0,3}$  &$\set{1,4}$ \ck  &$\set{0,3}$  \\                              
          $\set{1,3}$  &$\set{2,4}$ \ck  &$\set{1,3}$  \\                              
          $\set{2,3}$  &$\set{3,4}$ \ck &$\set{2,3}$  \\                              
        \end{tabular}
        \qquad
        \begin{minimizetable}{5}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
          \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
          \cline{2-2}
          &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
          \cline{3-3}
          \ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
          \cline{4-4}
          \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
          \cline{1-4}      
        \end{minimizetable}
        \qquad
        \equivclasses{%
          \eclass_{1,0} &=\set{0,1,2} \\
          \eclass_{1,1} &=\set{3} \\
          \eclass_{1,2} &=\set{4}
        }
      \end{center}
      Divide the $1$-equivalence classes into $2$-equivalence classes.
      \begin{center} \small
        \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
          \cline{2-3}
          $\set{0,1}$  &$\set{1,2}$  &$\set{0,1}$  \\
          $\set{0,2}$  &$\set{1,3}$ \ck  &$\set{0,2}$  \\
          $\set{1,2}$  &$\set{2,3}$ \ck &$\set{1,2}$  \\
        \end{tabular}
        \qquad
        \begin{minimizetable}{5}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
          \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
          \cline{2-2}
          \ck    &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
          \cline{3-3}
          \ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
          \cline{4-4}
          \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
          \cline{1-4}      
        \end{minimizetable}
        \qquad
        \equivclasses{%
          \eclass_{2,0} &=\set{0,1} \\
          \eclass_{2,1} &=\set{2} \\
          \eclass_{2,2} &=\set{3} \\
          \eclass_{2,3} &=\set{4}
        }
      \end{center}
      Divide the $2$-equivalence classes into $3$-equivalence classes.
      \begin{center} \small
        \begin{tabular}{r|ll}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
          \cline{2-3}
          $\set{0,1}$  &$\set{1,2}$ \ck  &$\set{0,1}$  \\
        \end{tabular}
        \qquad
        \begin{minimizetable}{5}
          \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
          \cline{1-1}
          \ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
          \cline{2-2}
          \ck    &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
          \cline{3-3}
          \ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
          \cline{4-4}
          \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
          \cline{1-4}      
        \end{minimizetable}
        \qquad
        \equivclasses{%
          \eclass_{3,0} &=\set{0} \\
          \eclass_{3,1} &=\set{1} \\
          \eclass_{3,1} &=\set{2} \\
          \eclass_{3,2} &=\set{3} \\
          \eclass_{3,3} &=\set{4}
        }
      \end{center}
      No additional splitting happens on the next iteration;
      indeed, there is nothing left to split.
      The process stops.
      The minimized machine has five states; it is the machine we started with.    
    \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise} \label{exer:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm}
    Verify the final sentence of \cref{lem:CorrectnessOfMooresAlgorithm} by
    showing that the $\sim$ relation is equal to $\sim_{n}$ for some
    $n$ less than or equal to the number of states in the machine.
    \textit{Hint:} to show that there must be a distinguishing
    string of that size you can use the Pumping lemma.
  \end{exercise}
  
  \begin{exercise} \label{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}
    Verify \cref{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}.
    \begin{exparts}
    \item Verify that each $\sim_{n}$ is an equivalence relation between
      states.
    \item Verify that $\sim$ is an equivalence.
    \end{exparts}
    \begin{answer}
      \begin{exparts}
        \item
        We must show that it is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.
        Fix some~$n$.
        Obviously any state is $n$-indistinguishable from itself, for any~$n$.
        Symmetry is also clear.
        So suppose $q_0\sim_{n} q_1$ and  $q_1\sim_{n} q_2$.
        Let $\sigma$ be a string of length less than or equal to~$n$.
        If $\sigma$ takes the machine from $q_0$ to an accepting state then
        because of $q_0\sim_{n} q_1$ it also takes the machine from
        $q_1$ to an accepting state.
        Because of $q_1\sim_{n} q_2$ it also takes the machine from
        $q_2$ to an accepting state.
        The same holds for non-accepting states.
      \end{exparts}
    \end{answer}
  \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language $\lang$ accepted by some Finite State machine, let
$\operatorname{rank}(\lang)$ be the smallest number~$n\in\N$
such that $\lang$ is
accepted by a Finite State machine with $n$ states.
Prove that for every~$n$ there is a language with that rank.
\begin{answer}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{minimization of a Finite State machine|)}\index{Finite State machine!minimization|)}







% =============================================
\section{Pushdown machines}\index{Pushdown machine|(}\index{Pushdown automata|(}
% "Stacks are overrated" Guy Steele https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=agw-wlHGi0E
%<*discussion:StackIntroi>
A Finite State machine cannot accept the language of balanced parentheses.
So it is not powerful enough to use, for instance,
if you want to decide whether input strings are valid programs in a
modern programming language.
The natural data structure to handle nested parentheses is a stack.
We will next see a machine type consisting of a Finite State
machine attached to a pushdown stack.
%</discussion:StackIntroi>

A stack\index{stack} 
is like a list in that the data is lined up in a row.
However, with a stack you don't have random access to the elements.
It is like the resturant dish dispenser shown below on the left.
When you take one dish off the top then a spring pushes the 
remaining dishes up, so you get access to the next one.
The reverse happens when you pop a new dish on; the spring gets compressed
so all the old dishes side down and
the new dish becomes the only one that is accessible.\index{stack!push}\index{stack!pop}

Similarly, you can push a piece of data onto the top of our
stack device, or you can pop the top element.
We say that this stack is 
LIFO:~Last-In, First-Out.\index{LIFO stack}\index{last in, first out (LIFO) stack}\index{stack!LIFO, Last-In, First-Out}

%<*discussion:StackIntroii>
Below on the right is a sequence of views of a stack data structure.
It starts with three characters on the stack, \str{g3}, \str{g0}, and~\str{g2}.
We push \str{g1} on the stack, and then \str{g0}.
At this point, although \str{g1} is on the stack we don't have immediate
access to it.
To get at \str{g1} we  must first pop off 
\str{g0}, as in the last stack shown.
%</discussion:StackIntroii>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.1in]{automata/pix/dishes.jpg}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/machine/machine02.pdf}}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/machine/machine03.pdf}}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/machine/machine04.pdf}}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/machine/machine05.pdf}}
\end{center}
Once something is popped, it is gone.
The machine could have a state whose intuitive meaning is,
say, that we just popped~$\str{g0}$, but because there are finitely
many states that strategy has limits.

So, like a Turing machine tape, a stack is unbounded storage.
But it has restrictions that the tape does not.


% ...............................................
\subsection{Definition}
To define these machines
we will extend the definition of Finite State machines, starting
with deterministic machines.

%<*def:PushdownMachine>
\begin{definition}
A \definend{Pushdown machine}\index{Pushdown machine}\index{Pushdown automata}
has a finite set of \definend{states} $Q=\set{q_0,\ldots\,q_{n-1}}$
including a \definend{start state}~$q_0$\index{start state!Pushdown machine}
and a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of \definend{accepting states},\index{accepting state!Pushdown machine}
a nonempty 
\definend{input alphabet}~$\Sigma$\index{alphabet!Pushdown machine}\index{Pushdown machine!input alphabet}
and a nonempty \definend{stack alphabet}~$\Gamma$,\index{stack!alphabet}\index{Pushdown machine!stack alphabet}
as well as a 
\definend{transition function}\index{transition function!Pushdown machine}\index{Pushdown machine!transition function}
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times (\Sigma\cup\set{\str{B},\varepsilon})\times \Gamma}{Q\times\kleenestar{\Gamma}}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:PushdownMachine>

\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine03.pdf}
\end{center}

%<*discussion:StackMachineTransitionsi>
We assume that the stack alphabet~$\Gamma$ contains the 
character that we use to mark the stack bottom,~$\bot$.\footnote{%
  Read that character aloud as ``bottom.''}\index{bottom@bottom, $\bot$}\index{stack!bottom@bottom, $\bot$}
The rest of $\Gamma$ is \str{g0}, \str{g1}, etc.
We also assume that the tape alphabet~$\Sigma$ does not contain the
blank,~\str{B}, or the character~$\varepsilon$.\footnote{%
  The definition allows $\varepsilon$ to appear in two separate places,
  as the second component
  of $\Delta$'s inputs and 
  also as the empty string, from $\kleenestar{\Gamma}$.
  However, one of those is in the inputs and the other is in the outputs
  so it isn't ambiguous.} 
%</discussion:StackMachineTransitionsi>

%<*discussion:StackMachineTransitionsii>
The transition function describes how these machines act.\index{Pushdown machine!transition function}
For the input
$\sequence{q_i,s,g_j}\in Q\times(\Sigma\cup\set{\str{B},\varepsilon})\times\Gamma$
there are two cases.
When the character~$s$ is an element of $\Sigma\cup\set{B}$
then an instruction
$\Delta(q_i,s,g_j)=\sequence{q_k,\gamma}$
applies when the machine is in state~$q_i$ with the tape head reading~$s$ 
and with the character~$g_j$ on top of the stack.
If there is no such instruction then the computation halts, with the
input string not accepted.
If there is such an instruction then
the machine does this:~(i)~the read head moves one cell to the right,
(ii)~the machine pops $g_j$ off the stack
and pushes the characters of the string
$\gamma=\sequence{g_{i_0},\ldots\,g_{i_m}}$ onto the stack in the order
from $g_{i_m}$ first to $g_{i_0}$ last,
and (iii)~the machine enters state $q_k$.
The other case for the input $\sequence{q_i,s,g_j}$
is when the character $s$ is $\varepsilon$.
Everything is the same except that the tape head does not move.
(We use this case to manipulate the stack without consuming any input.) 
%</discussion:StackMachineTransitionsii>

As with Finite State machines, Pushdown machines don't write to the tape but
only consume the tape characters.
However, unlike Finite State machines they can fail to
halt, see \cref{exer:PDANoHalt}.

%<*discussion:StackMachineTransitionsiii>
The starting configuration has the machine in state~$q_0$,
reading the first character
of~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$,
and with the stack containing only~$\bot$.
A machine accepts its input~$\sigma$ if, after starting
in its starting configuration and after scanning all of~$\sigma$,
it eventually enters an accepting state $q\in F$.

Notice that at each step the machine pops a character off the stack.
If we want to leave the stack unchanged then as part of the instruction
we must push that character back on.
In addition, if the machine reaches a configuration where 
the stack is empty then it will lock up and be unable to perform any more
instructions.\footnote{%
  An alternative to the final state definition of acceptance we are using is
  to define that a machine accepts its input if after consuming that input,
  it empties the stack.
  The definitions are equivalent in that a string is accepted by
  either type of machine if it is accepted by the other.}\index{Pushdown machine!halting}
%</discussion:StackMachineTransitionsiii>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PDSAcceptBalencedParens}
This grammar generates the language of balanced parentheses.
\begin{equation*}
\text{S \produces{} \trm{[} S \trm{]} \syntaxor SS \syntaxor \emptystring}
\qquad
  \lang_{\text{BAL}}=\set{\varepsilon,
    \str{[]},
    \str{[[]]},
    \str{[][]},
    \str{[[][]]},
    \str{[[[]]]}, \ldots}
\end{equation*}
The Pumping lemma shows that no Finite State machine accepts this language. 
But it is accepted by a
Pushdown machine.
%<*ex:BalancedParensAcceptedPushdown>
This machine has
states $Q=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$ with accepting states $F=\set{q_1}$,
and languages $\Sigma=\set{\text{\str{[}},\text{\str{]}}}$
and $\Gamma=\set{\text{\str{g0}},\bot}$.
The table gives its transition function~$\Delta$,
with the instructions numbered for ease of reference.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|ll}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Instr no}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Input}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Output}}  \\
  \hline
  $0$  &$q_o,\text{\str{[}},\bot$             &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}$\bot$'}$ \\
  $1$  &$q_o,\text{\str{[}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0g0}'}$ \\
  $2$  &$q_o,\text{\str{]}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\varepsilon$ \\
  $3$  &$q_o,\text{\str{]}},\bot$             &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $4$  &$q_o,\text{\str{B}},\bot$             &$q_1,\varepsilon$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
It keeps a running tally of the number of
\str{[}'s minus the number of \str{]}'s, as 
the number of \str{g0}'s on the stack.
This computation starts with the input \str{[[]][]}
and ends in an accepting state.
%</ex:BalancedParensAcceptedPushdown>
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine06.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine07.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine08.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine09.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine10.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine11.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine12.pdf} \\
    7 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine13.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PDAPalindromesMithMidMarker}
Recall that a palindrome
is a string that reads the same forwards and
backwards, $\sigma=\reversal{\sigma}$.
%<*ex:PalindromesUsingMiddleMarkeri>
This language of palindromes uses a~\str{c} character as a middle marker.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_{\text{MM}}
  =\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}}\suchthat
   \text{$\sigma=\tau\concat\str{c}\concat\reversal{\tau}$ for some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$}}
\end{equation*}
When the Pushdown machine below
is reading~$\tau$ it 
pushes characters onto the stack; 
\str{g0} when it reads~\str{a} and \str{g1} when it reads~\str{b}.
That's state~$q_0$.
When the machine hits the middle \str{c}, it reverses.
It enters $q_1$ and starts popping; 
when reading~\str{a} it checks that the popped character is~\str{g0},
and 
when reading~\str{b} it checks that what popped is~\str{g1}.
If the machine hits the stack bottom at the same moment that the input
runs out, then it goes into the accepting state~$q_3$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|ll}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Instr no}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Input}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Output}}  \\
  \hline
  $0$  &$q_0,\text{\str{a}},\bot$             &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}$\bot$'}$ \\
  $1$  &$q_0,\text{\str{b}},\bot$             &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}$\bot$'}$ \\
  $2$  &$q_0,\varepsilon,\bot$                &$q_3,\varepsilon$ \\
  $3$  &$q_0,\text{\str{a}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $4$  &$q_0,\text{\str{a}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $5$  &$q_0,\text{\str{b}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $6$  &$q_0,\text{\str{b}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $7$  &$q_0,\text{\str{c}},\text{\str{g0}}$   &$q_1,\text{`\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $8$  &$q_0,\text{\str{c}},\text{\str{g1}}$   &$q_1,\text{`\str{g1}'}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\hspace{2em}
\begin{tabular}{r|ll}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Instr no}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Input}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Output}}  \\
  \hline
  $9$  &$q_1,\text{\str{a}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_1,\varepsilon$ \\
  $10$ &$q_1,\text{\str{a}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $11$ &$q_1,\text{\str{a}},\bot$             &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $12$ &$q_1,\text{\str{b}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $13$ &$q_1,\text{\str{b}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\varepsilon$ \\
  $14$  &$q_1,\text{\str{b}},\bot$            &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $15$ &$q_1,\text{\str{B}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $16$ &$q_1,\text{\str{B}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $17$ &$q_1,\text{\str{B}},\bot$             &$q_3,\varepsilon$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
%</ex:PalindromesUsingMiddleMarkeri>
This computation has the machine accept \str{bacab}.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine25.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine26.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine27.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine28.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine29.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine30.pdf} \\
    6 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine31.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{example}


\begin{remark}
Stack machines are often used in practice, particularly for
running hardware.
% The familiar printer file format PDF is based on the PostScript language,
% which is based on a pushdown stack.
Here is a `Hello World' program in the PostScript printer language.
\begin{lstlisting}
/Courier               % name the font
20 selectfont          % font size in points, 1/72 of an inch 
72 500 moveto          % position the cursor
(Hello world!) show    % stroke the text
showpage               % print the page
\end{lstlisting}
The interpreter pushes \str{Courier} on the stack, and
then on the second line pushes \str{20}
on the stack.
It then executes \str{selectfont}, which pops two things off the stack
to set the font name and size.
After that it moves the current point, and places the text on the page.
Finally, it draws that page to paper.

This language is very fast and makes efficient use of the machine resources.
But it is suited to
situations where the code is written by a program, such as a word processor or
\LaTeX{}, than to situations where a person is writing it.
\end{remark}



% .......................
\subsection{Nondeterministic Pushdown machines}\index{nondeterministic Pushdown machine|(}\index{Pushdown machine!nondeterministic|(}
%<*discussion:NondeterminsticPushdown>
To get nondeterminism
we alter the definition in two ways.
The first is minor: we don't need the input characterblank~\str{B}
as a nondeterministic machine can guess when
the input string ends.

The second alteration changes the
transition function~$\Delta$.
We now allow the same input to give different outputs,
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times (\Sigma\cup\set{\varepsilon})\times \Gamma}{\powerset({Q\times\kleenestar{\Gamma}})}$.
(If the set of outputs is empty then we take the machine to freeze,
resulting in a computation that does not accept the input.)
As always with nondeterminism, 
we can conceptualize this either as that the 
the computation evolves as a tree or as that the machine chooses one
of the outputs.
%</discussion:NondeterminsticPushdown>

\begin{example} \label{ex:PDAForPalindromes}
This grammar generates the language of all palindromes\index{palindrome}
over $\kleenestar{\B}$.
\begin{equation*}
\text{P \produces{} \emptystring \syntaxor \trm{0} \syntaxor \trm{1} \syntaxor \trm{0}P\trm{0} \syntaxor \trm{1}P\trm{1}}
\qquad
\lang_{\text{PAL}}=\set{\varepsilon, \str{0},\str{1},\str{00},\str{11},\str{000},\str{010},\str{101},\str{111},\ldots}
\end{equation*}
This language is not accepted by any Finite State machine,
but it is accepted by a Pushdown machine.

%<*ex:NondeterministicPushDownAcceptsPalindromes>
This machine has
$Q=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$ with accepting states $F=\set{q_2}$,
and languages $\Sigma=\B$
and $\Gamma=\set{\text{\str{g0}},\str{g1},\bot}$.

% The transition function~$\Delta$ is below.
During its first phase it puts~\str{g0}
on the stack when it reads the input~\str{0} and puts~\str{g1} on the stack
when it reads~\str{1}.
During the second phase,
if it reads~\str{0} then it only proceeds
if the popped stack character is~\str{g0}
and if it reads~\str{1} then it only proceeds if it popped~\str{g1}.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|ll}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Instr no}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Input}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Output}}  \\
  \hline
  $0$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\bot$             &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}$\bot$'}$ \\
  $1$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\bot$             &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}$\bot$'}$ \\
  $2$  &$q_o,\varepsilon,\bot$                &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $3$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $4$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $5$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $6$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_0,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $7$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $8$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g0}\str{g1}'}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\hspace{3em}
\begin{tabular}[t]{r|ll}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Instr no}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Input}}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Output}}  \\
  \hline
  $9$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $10$ &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g1}\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $11$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g0}}$   &$q_1,\text{`\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $12$  &$q_o,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $13$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g0}}$   &$q_1,\text{`\str{g0}'}$ \\
  $14$  &$q_o,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\text{`\str{g1}'}$ \\
  $15$  &$q_1,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g0}}$  &$q_1,\varepsilon$ \\
  $16$  &$q_1,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_1,\varepsilon$ \\
  $17$ &$q_1,\text{\str{0}},\text{\str{g0}}$   &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
  $18$  &$q_1,\text{\str{1}},\text{\str{g1}}$  &$q_2,\varepsilon$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
How does the machine know when to change from phase one to two?
It is nondeterministic\Dash it guesses.
For instance,
compare instructions~$3$ and~$7$,
which show the same input associated with
two different outputs.
%</ex:NondeterministicPushDownAcceptsPalindromes>

Here the machine
accepts the string \str{0110}.
In the calculation it 
uses instructions $0$, $9$, $16$, and~$17$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine14.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine15.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine16.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine17.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine18.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
Here is the machine accepting \str{01010} using instructions
$0$, $4$, $12$, $16$, and~$17$.
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine19.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine20.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine21.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine22.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine23.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{automata/asy/machine/machine24.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \end{tapesequence}
  
The nondeterminism is crucial.
In the first example, 
after step~$1$ the machine is in state~$q_0$, is reading a~\str{1},
and the character that will be popped off the stack is~\str{g0}.
Both instructions~$3$ and~$9$ apply to that configuration.
But, applying instruction~$3$
would not lead to the machine accepting the input string.
The computation shown instead applies instruction~$9$, going to
state~$q_1$, whose intuitive meaning is that the machine switches from
pushing to popping.

We have given two mental models of nondeterminism.
One is that the machine guesses when to switch, and that for this
even-length string making that switch halfway through is the right guess.
We say the string is accepted because there exists a guess that is correct,
that ends in acceptance.
(That there exist incorrect guesses is not relevant.)

Taking the other view of nondeterminism omits guessing and instead
sees the computuation as a tree.
In one branch the machine applies instruction~$3$ and in another
it applies instruction~$9$.
By definition, for this machine the string is accepted
because there is at least one accepting
branch (the above table of the sequence of configurations
shows the tree's accepting branch).

Input strings with odd length are different.
In the language of guessing, the machine needs to guess
that it must switch from pushing to popping at the middle character,
but it must not push anything onto the stack since that thing would
never get popped off.
Instead, when instruction~$12$ pops the top character~\str{g1}
off the stack, as all instructions do when they are executed,
it immediately pushes it back on.
The net effect is that in this turn around from pushing to popping
the stack is unchanged.
\end{example}

Recall that deterministic Finite State machines can do
any jobs that nondeterministic ones can do.
The palindrome result shows that for Pushdown machines the situation is
different. 
While nondeterministic Pushdown machines can accept the langauge of
palindromes, that job cannot be done by deterministic Pushdown machines.
So for Pushdown machines, nondeterminism changes what can be done.

Intuitively, Pushdown machines are between Turing machines
and Finite State machines in that
they have a kind of unbounded read/write memory, but it is limited.
We've proved that they are more powerful than Finite State machines
because they can recognize the language of balanced parentheses.

There is a relevant result that we will mention but not prove:~there 
are jobs that Turing machines can do but that no
Pushdown machine can do.
One is the decision problem for the language
$\set{\sigma\concat\sigma\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$.
The intuition is that this language contains strings such
as \str{1010}, \str{10101010}, etc.
A Pushdown machine can push the characters onto the stack, as
it does for the language of balanced parentheses, but then to check that
the second half matches the first it would need to pop them off
in reverse order.\footnote{%
  Another way to tell that the set of languages accepted
  by an nondeterministic Pushdown machine is a strict subset of the set
  of languages accepted by a Turing machine is to note that there is
  no Halting Problem for Pushdown machines.
  We can write a program that inputs a string and a 
  Pushdown machine, and decides whether it is accepted.
  But of course we cannot write such a program for Turing machines.
  Since the languages differ and since anything computed by a Pushdown
  machine can be computed by a Turing machine, the languages of Pushdown
  machines must be a strict subset.}

The diagram below summarizes.
The box encloses all languages of bitstrings, all subsets of
$\kleenestar{\B}$.
The nested sets enclose those languages accepted by some Finite State
machine, or some Pushdown machine, etc.
\begin{center} \small
  % \mbox{Languages over $\B$:\ }
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/pda/pda00.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{tabular}{rl}
    \tabulartext{Class}  &\tabulartext{Machine type} \\
    \hline
    $A$ &Finite State, including nondeterministic\rule{0em}{2.15ex} \\
    $B$ &Pushdown \\
    $C$ &nondeterministic Pushdown \\
    $D$ &Turing \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
\index{nondeterministic Pushdown machine|)}\index{Pushdown machine!nondeterministic|)}




% .........................................
\subsection{Context free languages}\index{context free!language|(}\index{language!context free|(}
In the section on Grammars we restricted our attention
to production rules where the head consists of a single nonterminal, such
as \mbox{S \produces{} \trm{c}S}.
An example of a rule where the head is not of that form is
\mbox{\trm{c}S\trm{b} \produces{} \trm{a}S}.
With this rule we can substitute for~S only if it is
preceded by~\str{c} and followed by~\str{b}.
A grammar with rules of this type is called \definend{context sensitive}
because substitutions can only be done in a context.

If a language has a grammar in which all the rules are of the first type,
of the type we described in Chapter~III's \Cref{sec:Grammars},
then it is a \definend{context free} language.
Most modern programming languages are context free,
including C, Java, Python, and Scheme.
So grammars that are context sensitive, 
without the restriction of being required to be context free,
are much less common in practice.

We will state, but not prove,
the connection with this section:~a
language is accepted by some nondeterministic Pushdown machine
if and only if it has a context free grammar.
\index{context free!language|)}\index{language!context free|)}




\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:PDANoHalt}
Produce a Pushdown Automata that does not halt.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Produce a Pushdown machine to accept each language over
$\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{c}\str{b}^{2n}\suchthat n\in\N}$
\item
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{c}\str{b}^{n-1}\suchthat n>0}$
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a Pushdown machine that accepts
$\set{\str{0}\concat\tau\concat\str{1}\suchthat \tau\in\kleenestar{\B}}$.
\begin{answer}
This is a regular language so you don't need the stack.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the Pushdown Automata in \cref{ex:PDSAcceptBalencedParens}.
\begin{exparts}
\item
It has an asymmetry in the definition.
In line~$3$ it specifies that if there are too many \str{]}'s
then the machine should go to the error state~$q_2$.
But there is no line specifying what to do if there are too many
\str{[}'s.
Why is it not needed?
\item
Prove that this machine accepts the language of balanced parentheses
defined by the grammar.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a Pushdown machine that accepts
$\set{\str{a}^{2n}\str{b}^{n}\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\Cref{ex:PDAForPalindromes} discusses the view of a nondeterministic
computation as a tree.
Draw the tree for that machine these inputs.
\begin{exparts*}
\item \str{0110}
\item \str{01010}
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The grammar \mbox{Q \produces{} 0Q0 | 1Q1 | $\varepsilon$} generates a
different language of palindromes than the grammar in
\cref{ex:PDAForPalindromes}.
What is this language?
\begin{answer}
It is the language of even-length palindromes,
$\set{\tau\concat\reversal{\tau}\suchthat \tau\in\kleenestar{\B}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Write a context-free grammar for
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{c}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}}\suchthat n\in\N}$,
the language where the number of \str{a}'s before the \str{b} is the same
as the number of \str{c}'s after it.
\begin{answer}
\begin{grammar}
X \produces{} \trm{a}X\trm{c}
              \syntaxor b
\end{grammar}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Find a grammar that generates the language
$\set{\sigma\concat \str{b}\concat\reversal{\sigma} \suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}}$.
\begin{answer}
\begin{grammar}
T \produces{} \trm{a}T\trm{a}
              \syntaxor \trm{b}T\trm{b}
              \syntaxor \trm{b}
\end{grammar}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The grammar \mbox{Q \produces{} 0Q0 | 1Q1 | $\varepsilon$} generates a
different language of palindromes than the grammar in
\cref{ex:PDAForPalindromes}.
What is this language?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/60238/78536
Find a grammar for the language over $\sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$
consisting of palindromes that contain at most three \str{c}'s.
\hint~Use two nonterminals, with one for the case of not adjoining \str{c}'s.
\begin{answer}
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} \trm{a}S\trm{a} \syntaxor \trm{b}S\trm{b} 
   \syntaxor \trm{c}T\trm{c} 
   \syntaxor \trm{a} \syntaxor \trm{b} \syntaxor \trm{c} \syntaxor \emptystring

T \produces{} \trm{a}T\trm{a} \syntaxor \trm{b}T\trm{b} 
  \syntaxor \trm{a} \syntaxor \trm{b} \syntaxor \trm{c} \syntaxor \emptystring
\end{grammar}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:PanindromesNotRegularLang}
Show that the language
of all palindromes from \cref{ex:PDAForPalindromes}
is not accepted by any Finite State machine.
\hint~you can use the Pumping lemma.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that a string $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ is a palindrome
$\sigma=\reversal{\sigma}$ if and only if it is generated by the grammar
given in \cref{ex:PDAForPalindromes}.
\hint~Use induction in both directions.
\begin{answer}
One direction is that if a string~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$
is generated by the grammar then it is a palindrome $\sigma=\reversal{\sigma}$.
We do induction on the grammar's structure.
The basis step is that the string is either empty $\sigma=\varepsilon$,
or of length one~$\sigma=\str{0}$ or $\sigma=\str{1}$.
All three cases are evidentally palindromes.
For the inductive step we assume the length of the
string~$\lh{\sigma}$ is greater than one and that the statement is true for all
shorter strings, all~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$ with $\lh{\tau}<\lh{\sigma}$.
In the case that $\sigma=\str{0}\tau\str{0}$, the string~$\sigma$ is a
palindrome by the inductive hypothesis that $\tau$ is a palindrome.
The case that $\sigma=\str{1}\tau\str{1}$ is similar.

The other direction is that if the string is a palindrome
$\sigma=\reversal{\sigma}$ then it is generated by the grammar.
We can use induction on the length~$\lh{\sigma}$.
If the length of $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ is~$0$ then the string
is empty, $\sigma=\varepsilon$.
In this case it is a palindrome and is also derived from the grammar since
\mbox{P \produces{} \emptystring} is one of the listed production rules.
If the length of $\sigma$ is~$1$ then either $\sigma=\str{0}$ or
$\sigma=\str{1}$, and these two are both palindromes and also derived from
the grammar.
Finally, assume that $\lh{\sigma}>1$ and that the statement is true for all
strings $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$ of length less than~$\sigma$.
There are two cases.
If $\sigma$'s first character is~$0$ then because $\sigma$ is a palindrome
it has the form $\sigma=\str{0}\tau\str{0}$, where $\tau$ is a palindrome.
The induction hypothesis applies to give that $\tau$ is derived from the
grammar.
But then so is $\sigma$, using the production 
\mbox{P \produces{} \trm{0}P\trm{0}}.
The case that $\sigma$'s first character is~$\str{1}$ works the same way. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the set of pushdown automata is countable.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that any language accepted by a pushdown automata is accepted by some
Turing machine.
\end{exercise}

There is a Pumping lemma for Context Free languages:~if $\lang$ is
Context Free then it has a pumping length~$p\geq 1$ 
such that any $\sigma\in\lang$ with $\lh{\sigma}\geq p$
decomposes into five parts
$\sigma=\alpha\concat\beta\concat\gamma\concat\delta\concat\zeta$
sibject to the conditions (i)~$\lh{\alpha\beta\gamma}\leq p$,
(ii)~$\lh{\beta\delta}\geq 1$,
and
(iii)~$\alpha\beta^n\gamma\delta^n\zeta\in\lang$ for all $n\in\N$.
\begin{exparts}

Use it to show that $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\str{c}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$
is not Context Free.

Context Free.
\end{exparts}
% \begin{answer} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/276/78536
% \begin{exparts}

% \end{exparts}

\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For both Turing machines and Finite State machines, after we gave an informal
